That first run is usually a helper run: `materialize` and `restore-complete` parse content the session supplied, such as its archives, its git repositories, or its restored snapshot, as the slot UID, and setup commands, which are session code, run after them at bind and before the launch ([SIR-38](../0145-session-isolation/change.md#requirements)). From that run on the supervisor runs as the slot UID beside code or content the session controls. The restore at bind writes the seed before any helper run of the bind, so this point leaves it unaffected. For each passed descriptor it checks the device and mount ID with `statx` against the broker root's recorded identity, the file type against the type it asked for, the access mode, under which an `O_PATH` descriptor for a regular file is never read as content and is recorded only as a metadata-only entry, and the size against the size limit of the volume it came from, `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` or `sessionSizeLimitBytes`, and for a restore it accepts only an `O_WRONLY` descriptor of size 0 with one link. It applies the device, mount ID, and type checks to every directory descriptor the walk opens itself beneath a passed one, with an inode match against the entry's `statx`, and the device and mount ID check to every `statx` result of the walk. For each reply it checks that the number of descriptors equals the number of zero error numbers, that no control message was truncated (`MSG_CTRUNC`), the reply ID, that no member is unknown, and a bound on the line length, and a request without a reply within its deadline, `1 s + 4 ms × n` for a request of `n` paths counted from the reply to the request ahead of it on the connection, makes the slot dead. A `statat` reply carries no descriptor, so the adapter checks only its shape and its device numbers against the broker root's, and a false answer can only leave that session's snapshot stale, as the one reply these checks accept can leave it wrong. Under gVisor's default mount a FIFO reports a device other than the broker root's, so the device check refuses it. The capture then records that FIFO as a metadata-only entry from its directory listing in place of leaving it out of the snapshot, and refuses every other entry that fails the check (IC-1, "Reading the slot's files"). In the spike of 2026-11-12 these checks refused 24 of 25 hostile replies under runc, gVisor, and gVisor's pod-share mount: a FIFO, `/dev/zero`, a `/tmp` file, a file on another volume with the same `st_dev`, which only the mount ID caught, a memory file, a sparse file of 1 TiB, a directory, a socket, write-only, read-write, and `O_PATH` descriptors, and for a restore an existing file, a FIFO, a `/tmp` file, and a read-only descriptor, together with malformed, flooded, and stalled replies. The reply that passed was a descriptor for another file in the same workspace. It can make only that session's own snapshot wrong, because the slot can write those bytes itself, and S4 does not detect it, because the adapter hashes the bytes it reads.
- S7. A slot reaches no other slot's files through a checkpoint, a restore, or an export. Each broker resolves paths only beneath its own slot's roots without following a symbolic link or crossing a mount, and S6's mount ID check refuses a descriptor from any other volume. The walk's own lookups keep the same confinement: it calls `statx` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW` only on single names that `getdents` returned, never on `.`, `..`, an empty name, or a name holding `/`, and it opens subdirectories with `openat2` and `RESOLVE_BENEATH \| RESOLVE_NO_XDEV \| RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS \| RESOLVE_NO_MAGICLINKS` relative to a checked directory descriptor (IC-1, "The walk"). A session can make only its own snapshot inconsistent or incomplete, for example by writing the barrier file itself or by making its files unreadable to their owner.
- S8. A session can learn from a platform chunk set only whether content its own pod holds, the store commit at READY or its runtime revision's assets, is in the contribution that describes it. No session writes to a set: only a never-launched pod contributes, and only for sources on its own warm list or its runtime's assets (S1). The byte counters of the pod's network interface, which every container of a pod reads under runc, gVisor, and Kata, the checkpoint timing, and the quota therefore show a session nothing about another session. A pod compromised before its first bind can store packs whose bytes do not match their chunk IDs, which S4 detects at restore, so the effect is failed restores of the sessions that reference them rather than a disclosure.

R5 and S1 to S4 apply to the packs that the `ExportOutputFile` stream writes for output files (IC-3). Those packs use the same key prefix, `/{tenant_id}/checkpoints/{session_id}/packs/{pack_id}`, and the same `checkpoint_packs` rows as a checkpoint's packs. S1's refusal covers their grants, S2's check runs on each output file's pack references, and S4's verification runs on each download of an output file (IC-5).

## Current state

- `ArchiveTree` (`pkg/adapter/workspace/tree.go`) walks the workspace and the `/sessions` tree, writes a tar, and compresses it with `compress/gzip`. The handler slices the stream into fixed-size tar chunks and uploads them one at a time against per-chunk presigned `PUT` grants (`streamChunks` and `uploadChunk` in `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go`).
- `ExtractTree` restores the stream and stops at a fixed 2 GiB (`maxExtractBytes` in `pkg/adapter/workspace/extract.go`).
- Derive copies every tar chunk object of the source checkpoint into the derived session's prefix ([§7.1 Normal Flow](../../spec/07_session-lifecycle.md#71-normal-flow) **Derive copy semantics:**), and [§12.5 Artifact Store](../../spec/12_storage-architecture.md#125-artifact-store) **Workspace deduplication.** states that a future content-addressed format must add reference counting to garbage collection and erasure.
- Retention keeps the latest 2 checkpoints per session (`pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointretention`), with legal-hold exemptions.
- An attempt whose deadline fires leaves a partial manifest, a Postgres row recording the tar chunks it uploaded, which the resume path reassembles into a tar prefix ([§10.1.7 preStop Hook Drain](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1017-prestop-hook-drain) **Reassembly on resume.**).
- The embedded `SIGSTOP` checkpoint path in `pkg/adapter/embeddedcheckpoint`, which stops the runtime process with `SIGSTOP` for a checkpoint and resumes it with `SIGCONT`, has no caller outside its own package and tests. §4.4.3 specifies it, and `pkg/checkpoint` maps its `embedded` checkpoint level to `consistent` on the strength of it. IC-9 removes the path.
- Under [`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md) and [`0118-agent-pod-layout`](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md), which this design reads as implemented, each slot runs in its own slot container under its own slot UID, with its own workspace and session volumes, which the slot container mounts whole at `/workspace` and `/session`. The adapter reaches a slot's files only through the slot's file-descriptor broker. The spool and the export staging, which revision 12 placed in the workspace volume beside the slots, sit on a separate volume that only the adapter mounts. A slot release ends the slot's processes, sweeps its files, runs the pool's cleanup commands, ends the processes and sweeps again, and launches nothing, and the next session's runtime is launched at its bind. The embedded deployment model no longer exists ([PL-2](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-2-removal-of-the-embedded-deployment-model)).
- Under [`0153-workspace-materialization`](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md), every git step on a slot repository runs as the slot UID in `lenny-materialize`, which the supervisor's worker runs, and the adapter runs no git on slot repositories.

## Starting points

The owner's pointers propose a copy-on-write snapshot, followed by an incremental, deduplicated archive of that snapshot. The table records what each pointer contributes here.

| Pointer | Fit for Lenny | Outcome |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| An atomic filesystem snapshot on ZFS, Btrfs, or thin volumes of LVM (the Linux Logical Volume Manager), taken before archiving | Requires a chosen node filesystem and privileged operations. Agent pods drop all Linux capabilities ([§13.1 Pod Security](../../spec/13_security-model.md#131-pod-security)), and the workspace is an `emptyDir` directory rather than a dataset or subvolume. | Deferred as a node-level provider (see "Node-level snapshots"). |
| A reflink copy with `cp --reflink=always` | `FICLONE` needs no privilege on XFS with `reflink=1` and on Btrfs. It cloned a 2 GiB file in 1.1 ms, and it was slower than a plain copy for 4 KiB files. On AWS it worked under runc on XFS and returned `EOPNOTSUPP` under gVisor and Kata (see "Benchmarks"). | Adopted for large changed files during the capture pass, when the reflink probe at adapter start finds it available. In practice that is runc only. |
| restic, BorgBackup, or Kopia for incremental, deduplicated archives | Each tool needs direct repository access with listing and deletion, plus repository locks and pruning. That conflicts with R5. Borg also needs Python and an SSH or local repository. | The techniques are adopted: content-defined chunking, deduplication, packs, and comparison against the parent snapshot's file metadata. The tools are not adopted. |
| Plain tar is the wrong tool at this frequency | Agreed. | Replaced by packs and a manifest. |
| An atomic snapshot keeps `.git` consistent, and `git bundle` or `git clone --mirror` otherwise | The quiesced capture pass is point-in-time for quiescing runtimes. A bundle needs git in the adapter image and re-reads history on each run. | Ordered capture of git metadata on the best-effort path (see "Git repositories"). Bundles are not adopted. |
| Timer and event triggers with a debounce | The gateway's periodic schedule and its existing triggers stay. | A background pre-copy between checkpoints is a later optimization. |
| restic or Kopia on the live folder works, but the result is not point-in-time | This is the pre-copy pass. | The capture pass closes the gap for quiescing runtimes. |

## IC-1: Capture in passes

A checkpoint runs a pre-copy pass, a capture pass, and an upload pass. The runtime is quiesced only during the capture pass.

### Reading the slot's files

The adapter reads and writes a slot's files only through that slot's file-descriptor broker ([SI decision D9](../0145-session-isolation/change.md#decisions)), on every pod, a pod with one slot included. No pass of this design resolves a path in a slot volume through the adapter's own mount, which [PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction) uses only for the start-up canary and for the removal step of a slot release. The walk's own lookups are relative to directory descriptors the broker passed, which stay in the slot container's mount view. All checkpoint logic stays in the adapter: the walk, the file index, the racy rule, chunking, cloning, the spool, and restore. The supervisor only opens, creates, stats, and watches entries and passes the descriptors.

- **Broker roots.** Before the pod reports READY, while no session has bound to the slot and no slot code runs, the adapter asks the broker for the slot's `workspace` and `session` roots, on `/workspace` and `/session`, and records each root descriptor's device and mount ID with `statx`. A passed descriptor's mount ID is that of the slot container's mount rather than the adapter's, and under gVisor's default mount and on Kata's shared file system its `st_dev` differs as well: the spike read mount ID 374 against the adapter's 322 and device `0:96` against `0:55` under gVisor, and the AWS run read device `0:57` and mount ID 186 against the adapter's `0:49` and 156 on Kata's shared file system. The adapter therefore compares identities only among results taken in the slot's view and checks every descriptor and every `statx` result against the broker root's recorded identity (S6).
- **Path translation.** A path the runtime names, such as a file part's `path`, is a slot container path. The adapter maps the prefix `/workspace/` to the `workspace` root and `/session/` to the `session` root and refuses any other prefix ([PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout), "Path translation").
- **Listing.** The adapter lists a directory with `getdents` on a directory descriptor: one the broker's `open` passed, or one the adapter opened beneath a passed descriptor (see "The walk"). That works for directories of mode 0700 and 0500, which the adapter's own mount refuses with `EACCES`, because the broker opens them as their owner.
- **Entries.** For content, it asks the broker for descriptors in batches of up to 253 paths, the `SCM_RIGHTS` limit. It uses `open`, which opens with `O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW | O_NONBLOCK | O_NOCTTY`, for regular files and directories, and `openpath`, which opens with `O_PATH`, for every entry whose `d_type` is neither, so the adapter never opens a FIFO. An entry whose `d_type` is `DT_UNKNOWN` gets `openpath` first, and `open` once `fstat` shows a regular file or a directory. The adapter reads a symbolic link's target with `readlinkat` on its parent directory's descriptor, or on its `O_PATH` descriptor with an empty path.
- **Confinement.** The broker resolves every path with `RESOLVE_BENEATH | RESOLVE_NO_XDEV | RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS | RESOLVE_NO_MAGICLINKS` from the broker root and refuses a final component that is empty, `.`, or `..`. The walk applies the same flags and the same name rule to its own lookups. No operation of this design therefore follows a symbolic link or crosses a mount, and the spikes refused every link they planted.
- **Paths.** Paths cross `CH-SUPERVISE` as raw-byte JSON strings, which the supervisor's parser keeps byte for byte. The adapter encodes them with its own encoder, because Go's `json.Marshal` replaces invalid UTF-8, and the manifest stores paths as byte strings (IC-2).
- **Reach.** The adapter reads every entry the slot's owner can open: files of mode 0600 and 0400, `mkstemp` files, a file an atomic save left at 0600, and directories of mode 0700 and 0500. A walk through the adapter's own mount refused every one of them.
- **Reserved names.** The walk and the tracker skip the names that [`0153-workspace-materialization`](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md) and [PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout) reserve at the top of `/workspace` for the platform: `.lenny-canary`, which the start-up sweep removes; `.lenny-build`; `.lenny-overlay-<id>`, the staging directory of an overlay in progress, recorded by its exact name; `.lenny-prestage`, where warm-up checkouts wait to be adopted at bind; and `.lenny-discard-<id>`, an unused warm-up tree renamed aside and awaiting deletion. None of them is part of the session's workspace. The capture also skips a directory named `lost+found` at the root of either tree, which an `ext4` volume such as a Kata `block-plain` volume holds at its root and which the release's emptiness check requires to be empty ([`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md#requirements), SIR-34). A restore never creates it or writes into it, and a download leaves it out, so a session that writes into it through the volume's group keeps those bytes out of its snapshots.
- **Helper runs.** The supervisor's worker runs one helper run or setup command at a time and keeps serving broker requests while it runs. A checkpoint that starts during a helper run waits for that run's `setupdone`, because the capture copy that precedes pre-copy is itself a helper run (see "Git repositories"). An export's reads proceed during a run.
- **Dead slots.** A dead slot has no supervisor, so the adapter cannot reach its files, and it has no direct path to fall back to. A checkpoint, an export, or a seal of the slot's session fails, and the session follows [§7.3 Retry and Resume](../../spec/07_session-lifecycle.md#73-retry-and-resume) from its latest snapshot, as `0145-session-isolation`, "SI-4" states. Through a preStop drain every supervisor keeps serving, so the eviction checkpoint reaches every live slot.

**Metadata-only entries.** The broker opens what the slot's owner can open, and these entries remain out of reach:

- A file its owner cannot read, such as one of mode 0000, yields only an `O_PATH` descriptor. The manifest records its type, mode, size, and `mtime` with the flag `metadata_only`, and no content.
- A directory its owner cannot list, such as one of mode 0300 or 0100, is recorded with its metadata and `metadata_only`, and its entries are not captured. Its owner cannot list it either without a `chmod`.
- A FIFO under gVisor's default mount reports a device other than the broker root's, so S6's device check refuses it. The capture records it from its directory listing with `metadata_only`, and a restore recreates it with `mkfifo`. The AWS re-validation found that before this rule each such FIFO was left out of the snapshot without an error.
- A socket is recorded and is not restored.
- A device node cannot exist, because `mknod` fails with `EPERM` in a slot container.
- Extended attributes are not captured. `fgetxattr` on a passed descriptor of a file of mode 0600 fails with `EACCES`, because the kernel checks the caller's access to the file, so capturing them would need a broker operation of its own. The stored format carries none.

The capture reports each `metadata_only` entry with a session warning event and the metric `lenny_checkpoint_metadata_only_paths_total`, under the rule of SI's O2, which now covers only what the slot's owner cannot open.

### The walk

The stat walk reads every entry's identity in parallel in the adapter, relative to directory descriptors in the slot's mount view, and asks the broker only for what the adapter may not do itself ([decision D19](#decisions)). The supervisor serves broker requests on one thread, so a walk that asked it for every entry was bound by that thread: on AWS the walk through `open` cost 2.6 µs per entry under runc against 0.57 µs directly, and adding walker threads past 4 gained nothing ("Re-validation through the broker").

1. The adapter opens the root of each of the session's trees through the broker's `open` and checks the descriptor against the pinned root (S6).
2. For each directory it holds a descriptor and lists the directory with `getdents` on it. It queues the names in chunks of 256, so the entries of one large directory are spread over several threads.
3. On each of N worker threads, 16 by default, it calls `statx` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW` and `STATX_BASIC_STATS | STATX_MNT_ID` on each name relative to the directory's descriptor. It accepts only single names that `getdents` returned, never `.`, `..`, an empty name, or a name that holds `/` or a NUL byte, and it refuses a result whose device or mount ID differs from the pinned root's, or that carries no mount ID.
4. It opens each subdirectory relative to its parent's descriptor with `openat2` and `RESOLVE_BENEATH | RESOLVE_NO_XDEV | RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS | RESOLVE_NO_MAGICLINKS`, applies S6's device, mount ID, and type checks to the new descriptor, and checks that its inode matches the entry's `statx`.
5. When a `statx` in a directory fails with `EACCES`, because the adapter's UID may not search that directory, the rest of that directory goes to the broker's `statat` in batches. When an `openat2` fails with `EACCES`, the subdirectory is opened through the broker's `open`. This fallback is per directory and on `EACCES` only. The adapter keeps at most `walkFallbackConcurrency` fallback requests outstanding per slot, a constant of 2, because 8 and 16 threads queueing fallback batches on the supervisor's one thread gave worst requests of 146 to 370 ms on Kata's shared file system, against 14 to 20 ms for a walk that sent its `statat` batches one directory at a time.
6. It reads symbolic link targets with `readlinkat` on the parent's descriptor.

A walk with no `EACCES` sends one broker request, the `open` of each tree's root, and its worst broker round trip on AWS was 0.1 to 0.5 ms. At 16 threads its cost per entry was 1.0 to 1.2 times the adapter's direct walk on every runtime: 0.53 to 0.56 µs under runc, 1.0 to 1.1 µs on a Kata block volume, 3.1 to 3.4 µs on gVisor's pod-share mount, 9.8 to 10.2 µs on gVisor's default mount, and 12.7 to 13.9 µs on Kata's shared file system. Missed changes and identity differences against the slot's own view were 0 on every runtime. A directory the adapter may not search costs about what `statat` costs, so the share of such directories sets the gain. A dedicated `fsGroup` on Kata `block-plain` pools makes directories of mode 0750 searchable by the adapter and halved the fallback in the permission test ([`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md), the Kata `block-plain` decision).

**Large-directory rules.** A directory with very many entries stays within the request deadlines and the pause budget:

- Every broker request carries at most 253 paths and 56 KiB of path bytes.
- A large directory spans many requests. The adapter pages `getdents` on its own descriptor, so the supervisor holds no state for a directory between requests.
- A request's deadline is `1 s + 4 ms × n` for `n` paths. It scales with the batch, so the size of a directory never lengthens one request's deadline.
- The walk as a whole is bounded by the pause budget. The estimate of IC-1, "Pause budget and convergence" counts the walk, and a walk whose estimate alone exceeds the budget sends the capture to the best-effort path rather than failing it.
- Under gVisor's default mount the longest `getdents` call on a directory of 1,000,000 entries took 140 to 190 ms in the local spike, so the estimate counts the first `getdents` of a large directory as part of the walk.
- A tracker whose queue overflows in a large directory falls back to the walk, and the event cap of IC-1, "Change tracker" does the same where the queue has no bound.

A flat directory of 1,000,000 entries paused 1.6 to 2.0 s under runc and on a Kata block volume and 3.4 to 4.3 s on gVisor's pod-share mount, and it fell to best effort on gVisor's default mount, at 9.1 to 10.6 s, and on Kata's shared file system, at 22 to 23 s. Such a workspace on those two file systems gets best-effort snapshots without a tracker.

**Cost.** The local spike measured the broker's per-file cost on an arm64 laptop ("Session isolation spikes"), and the AWS runs measured it at workspace scale ("Re-validation through the broker"). Batching is required, because one path per request cost 8 times as much under runc. Reading a changed 4 KiB file through the broker cost 8 µs under runc, 10 to 14 µs on a Kata block volume, 37 µs and 48 µs on gVisor's pod-share and default mounts, and 264 µs on Kata's shared file system, with 8 readers. The pause estimate measures per-file costs on the pod during pre-copy (see "Pause budget and convergence"), so it includes the broker's cost wherever it runs. The broker's `statat` operation ([decision D16](#decisions)) returns identities as integers without descriptors. The walk uses it as its fallback, and the identity pass after a restore is the same walk.

### Pre-copy

The adapter walks the session's trees, `/workspace` and `/session`, with the stat walk while the runtime runs (see "The walk"). Both trees go through the same passes, index, and clone rules, into one snapshot. For each entry the walk reads its identity: device, inode, size, `mtime` in nanoseconds, `ctime` in nanoseconds, and mode. It also records the link count. It compares that identity with the adapter's file index from the session's previous snapshot. When the change tracker is healthy, the pre-copy walk also cross-checks it: a file whose identity changed and that the tracker did not report marks the tracker degraded (see "Change tracker").

- A file whose identity matches a trusted index entry keeps the entry's chunk list without a read.
- Every other file is opened through the broker, read, split into chunks, and hashed. A chunk whose ID the dedup set holds is referenced. A new chunk is compressed and appended to the open pack, and a full pack is uploaded.
- The adapter records each file's identity with `fstat` on the passed descriptor before the read and checks it with `fstat` on the same descriptor after the read. A file whose identity changed during the read is marked dirty so that the capture pass reads it again. In the spike, `fstat` on a passed descriptor matched `lstat` on device, inode, size, mode, `mtime`, and `ctime` to the nanosecond, and the recheck caught a write during the read.

Pre-copy reads files with a bounded worker pool and keeps several pack uploads in flight. It holds no operation lock, so interrupts proceed. Packs it confirms stay usable when the attempt later fails (R3).

### Capture

On a quiescing runtime the capture pass runs under the cooperative handshake on `CH-MSGSOCK`.

1. The adapter takes the pod-wide operation lock and sends `checkpoint_request` with `deadlineMs` set to the pause budget.
2. After `checkpoint_ready`, the adapter builds the changed set: files whose identity differs from the index, dirty files, and racy entries (see "Change detection"). With a healthy change tracker, the adapter writes one byte to the session's barrier file, waits until the tracker has read the barrier's event, takes the tracker's dirty paths with the link groups they belong to (see "Change tracker"), and then reads the identity of those paths only, through the broker. Without a healthy tracker, the adapter walks the session's trees again with the stat walk, which also records directories, symbolic links, link groups, and modes.
3. The adapter decided before step 1 whether the capture fits the pause budget, from an estimate built from the slowest observed rates with its margin (see "Pause budget and convergence"). If the changed set found in step 2 exceeds that estimate by more than the budget allows, the adapter ends the capture without reading.
4. The adapter captures each changed file of either tree. A file of at least `reflinkMinBytes`, a constant of 1 MiB, that fits the session's remaining spool share is cloned into the spool with `FICLONE` from its passed descriptor when the reflink probe found reflink mode for the slot's volume, and copied into the spool with `copy_file_range` when the probe found copy mode. Every other changed file, a file larger than the remaining share included, is read in place, chunked, and hashed, and its new chunks are written to the spool uncompressed. The share bounds clones as it bounds copies, because the kubelet counts a clone at its full size (see "Spool").
5. The adapter sends `checkpoint_complete` with status `ok` and releases the lock. The snapshot's file list is complete at this point, and only the spooled bytes remain to upload.

The pause is the handshake, plus the tracker swap or the stat walk, plus the time to read or clone the changed files from local disk. Recently written files are usually in the page cache. Through the broker on the AWS nodes, the stat walk cost 0.53 to 0.56 µs per entry under runc, 1.0 to 1.1 µs on a Kata block volume, 3.1 to 3.4 µs on gVisor's pod-share mount, 9.8 to 10.2 µs on gVisor's default mount, and 12.7 to 13.9 µs on Kata's shared file system with its default cache mode. Reading a changed 4 KiB file cost 8 µs under runc, 10 to 14 µs on a Kata block volume, 37 to 48 µs under gVisor, and 264 µs on Kata's shared file system ("Re-validation through the broker"). "Benchmarks" holds the earlier direct figures.

### Spool

The spool of slot `n` is `/adapter/spool/<n>/checkpoint/` on `lenny-spool`, the spool volume, an `emptyDir` that only the adapter mounts, beside the slot's export clones and staging (SI decision D8, [PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout)). No slot container can see, rename, or fill it. `FICLONE` from a slot's volume into the spool volume needs both on one filesystem and Linux 5.18 or later, which compares the filesystems of the source and destination rather than their mounts. Disk-backed `emptyDir` volumes of one pod live on the kubelet's filesystem, so under runc on XFS with `reflink=1` the clone works: the spike cloned 200 MiB from a slot's volume into the spool volume in 36 to 98 µs, FIEMAP showed all 209,715,200 bytes shared, and a later overwrite of the original left the clone unchanged. Through a passed descriptor it cloned 12 files of 12, and a clone of 64 MiB used no spool bytes.

The reflink probe runs at adapter start for each pair of a slot's workspace or session volume and the spool volume, because a pair can differ from another pair on the same pod. Before the pod reports READY, while no session has bound to the slot, the adapter has the broker create a probe file in each slot volume with `mkfile`, writes it through the returned descriptor, and tries both calls into the spool. The Sweep that runs before READY removes the probe files ([PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction)). The probe records one of three modes:

- **Reflink mode.** `FICLONE` succeeds and FIEMAP reports the clone's extents as shared.
- **Copy mode.** `FICLONE` fails and `copy_file_range` succeeds. The probe claims no sharing, and the adapter counts every copy at its full size. Under gVisor `FICLONE` and `FICLONERANGE` return `EOPNOTSUPP`, FIEMAP returns `ENOTTY`, and `copy_file_range` made a full copy of 200 MiB in 41 to 48 ms, against 105 to 111 ms with `read` and `write`. Each copy added 200 MiB to the volume's use.
- **Read mode.** Neither call works, and a changed file reaches the spool only as chunks. On Kata `block-plain` each volume is a file system of its own inside the guest, so the probe finds read mode there.

On AWS the probe found reflink mode under runc on XFS, copy mode under gVisor on both mounts, where `copy_file_range` from a passed descriptor copied 256 MiB in 0.056 s, and read mode on Kata `block-plain`.

The pause estimate counts cloned bytes at the measured clone cost and copied bytes at the measured copy rate. A file in reflink or copy mode that does not fit the session's remaining spool share is read in place and chunked instead. The adapter removes spooled data once its pack is confirmed, and it accounts the spool as IC-7, "Spool cap" states.

**Eviction findings.** The AWS re-validation found three ways in which the kubelet evicted a pod for exceeding the spool volume's `sizeLimit`, which ends every slot's session. Revision 13 bounded only copy mode by the session's share, and it set `sizeLimit` equal to the cap.

- Under runc a reflink clone of a changed 4 GiB file was counted at its full size against `sizeLimit: 512Mi`, with almost no new disk, and the pod was evicted. The share now bounds every clone, and a larger file is read in place.
- Under gVisor's default mount, copies that filled the spool to exactly the cap, 4 × 128 MiB, were evicted. The cap left no room for anything else on the volume. The adapter now uses at most half of the spool's `sizeLimit`, and the other half is the spool headroom (IC-7, "Spool headroom").
- Under gVisor's default mount a fill at 75% of the cap was also evicted, because the kubelet saw more use on the node than the adapter counted, as "Kubernetes checks" found for that mount, and deletes did not return the space before the kubelet measured. Fills at 75% stayed under the limit for 90 s under runc, on gVisor's pod-share mount, and on a Kata block volume. The headroom of IC-7 covers the larger count on gVisor's default mount.

**Clone time of a large file under writes.** Under runc the reflink clone of the 4 GiB file under constant 1 MiB random rewrites took 11.0 s inside an 11.5 to 11.7 s pause on AWS, against 2.0 to 2.5 s in "Benchmarks", and the next checkpoint's clone of the same file took 0.5 s. The first clone probably pays for writing back the writer's dirty pages and for the file's fragmentation from the rewrites. The cause is an investigation owed ("Validation owed"). The adapter's estimate for that capture was 0.46 s, so the estimate's clone term did not foresee the cost, and the investigation covers how the estimate measures it.

### Upload

After the runtime resumes, the adapter chunks any cloned files, compresses the spooled chunks into packs, uploads them, uploads the manifest, and asks the gateway to finalize the snapshot. A failure in this pass fails the checkpoint without affecting the runtime, and the packs already confirmed stay usable by the next attempt.

### Pause budget and convergence

The capture pass is bounded by the pool's `checkpointPauseBudgetSeconds` (default 10), which is a time. A byte budget alone is not enough: on Kata's shared file system with a package install running, a changed set of 40 MiB in 10,000 files took 1.8 s to read, while a single changed 400 MiB file took 0.03 s to clone under runc. The adapter therefore estimates the capture time before it sends `checkpoint_request`:

```
estimate = change-source cost + changed files × per-file read cost + changed bytes ÷ read rate
```

- The change-source cost is the duration of the last stat walk, the first `getdents` of each large directory included, or the tracker's swap time plus its event backlog multiplied by its measured cost per event. The backlog is the byte count pending on the inotify descriptor, which `FIONREAD` reports, divided by the event size. A backlog that would carry the swap interval past the event cap counts as an overflow, and the change-source cost is then the walk's.
- The per-file cost, the read rate, and the clone cost are measured on the pod, so they reflect the isolation profile and the disk. The estimate uses the slowest value of each that the adapter has observed for the slot: in the attempt's pre-copy rounds and in the capture passes of the session's earlier checkpoints on the same pod ([decision D11](#decisions)). The change-source cost likewise takes the longest walk or swap of the attempt's rounds.
- The changed set is the set the pre-copy pass marked dirty, plus files the tracker reported after the pass.
- Bytes that the capture will clone with `FICLONE` count at the clone cost instead of the read rate.

**Margin.** The adapter sends `checkpoint_request` only when the estimate times `pauseEstimateMargin`, a constant of 1.5, is at most the pause budget. The owner asked for a conservative decision, because an estimate that turns out short keeps a quiesced runtime paused until the budget ends the capture.

The pool's `checkpointCaptureBudgetBytes` (default 512 MiB) stays as the spool cap. Pool admission rejects a `checkpointPauseBudgetSeconds` of 60 or more, because a quiescing runtime resumes on its own 60 seconds after `checkpoint_ready` and the capture would then no longer be point-in-time. When the estimate with its margin is over the pause budget, the adapter skips the handshake for this round, runs another pre-copy round, and estimates again, as live migration of a virtual machine does, up to the pool's `checkpointPreCopyRounds` (default 3) rounds. When the changed set found during the capture exceeds the estimate by more than the remaining budget, the adapter ends the capture with `checkpoint_complete` status `failed` and reason `precopy_required` and takes another round. Another round reduces only the changed set. When the change-source cost alone, which is the stat walk without a tracker, times the margin exceeds the budget, further rounds cannot bring the estimate under it, so the adapter skips them and captures on the best-effort path at once. Through the broker on AWS, with the parallel stat walk at 16 threads, Chromium's 565,000 entries paused 1.29 s under runc, 1.51 s on a Kata block volume, 2.55 s on gVisor's pod-share mount, 6.33 s on gVisor's default mount, and 9.44 s on Kata's shared file system. With the margin, the walk fits the 10 s default up to an estimate of 6.67 s, so the first four fit and Chromium's walk on Kata's shared file system takes the best-effort path, where it sat at the edge of the budget even without the margin, at 9.98 to 10.02 s with 4 threads. The tracker runs on Kata's shared file system under `auto`, so this applies only to a slot whose tracker is degraded or off. A walk through the broker's `open` alone had paused Chromium for 17.2 s on gVisor's default mount and 57.5 s on Kata's shared file system, and through `statat` alone for 10.2 s and 11.7 s, both of which took the best-effort path. A flat directory of 1,000,000 entries is the case in which the walk alone exceeds the budget on gVisor's default mount and on Kata's shared file system (see "The walk").

The benchmark harness decided with a byte estimate, which is the defect this formula corrects. Its timings bear out the terms of the formula. An unpaused stat walk predicted the paused walk that followed it with a median ratio of 1.13 and a worst case of 2.66 while files were being created. Under the tracker, the barrier wait took 0.2 to 0.4 s on runc and 3.5 to 6.1 s on gVisor during sustained file creation, because the tracker had a backlog of unread events at the pause (see "Benchmarks"). Reading the tracker's dirty set without a swap does not see that backlog, so the estimate takes it from the tracker's queue depth. The re-validation's supervisor-held tracker read events at 0.4 to 1.4 µs each in the adapter and kept the barrier wait at 1 to 6 ms on every runtime during sustained creation.

If the last round is still over budget, the adapter captures without quiescing, as on the best-effort path, and tags the snapshot `consistency: best-effort`. The session gets a usable checkpoint, and `lenny_checkpoint_consistency_downgrade_total` counts the event. When the pause budget expires during a capture, the adapter ends the capture, the runtime resumes, and the attempt continues on the best-effort path.

### Staggering across slots

At most one slot of a pod runs a checkpoint attempt at a time ([decision D20](#decisions), R14). The adapter holds a pod-wide checkpoint queue. An attempt that the gateway starts for a slot while another slot's attempt runs waits in the queue, in the order the attempts arrived, and starts when the running attempt ends, whether it succeeds or fails. The queue covers every trigger of a checkpoint: periodic, pre-scale-down, drain, CheckpointBarrier, eviction, and the seal.

- **Why.** Slots in one gVisor sandbox share its sentry, and two slots walking at once slowed each other's walk pause by 1.4 to 1.8 times on AWS: 10.9 to 11.5 s together against 6.1 to 6.5 s alone through `open` on the default mount, which put both over the budget. Under runc the same pair paused 0.68 to 0.72 s together against 0.54 to 0.65 s alone. Serializing the attempts keeps each slot's pause at the figure measured for a slot alone.
- **What waits.** The whole attempt waits: pre-copy, the capture pass, and the upload pass. The wait counts against the waiting attempt's `checkpointAttemptTimeoutSeconds` from its `CheckpointStart`, and an attempt whose deadline passes in the queue fails as a timed-out attempt does (IC-7). Exports are not queued. A capture pass pauses export reading, hashing, and compression as IC-3 states.
- **Drains.** On a preStop drain the eviction checkpoints of the pod's slots run one after another, each within `evictionCheckpointCapSeconds`. The pool-config admission webhook's agent-pod grace floor already counts one capped checkpoint for every slot, `maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod × evictionCheckpointCapSeconds + 30`, so the floor covers the serialized drain. The waits that depend on a pod's checkpoints scale the same way: the gateway waits for a pod's `CheckpointBarrierAck` up to `maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod × evictionCheckpointCapSeconds`, the BarrierAck floor checks that bound, and a gateway replica's preStop wait for the checkpoints in progress of the sessions it coordinates allows that bound for each pod it waits on (IC-7, "Drain caps").
- **Seal.** A seal waits in the queue as any attempt does, and the wait counts against the session's seal window as well as the attempt's deadline (IC-5, "Seal").
- **Disk watch.** The disk watch's walks of PL-5 measure volume use and capture nothing, so they do not join the queue.
- **Interrupts.** An interrupt of a slot whose attempt waits in the queue proceeds, because a queued attempt holds no operation lock.
- **Restores.** The queue does not cover restores. Two concurrent LLVM restores on one pod took 241 to 283 s against 47 to 132 s alone, because they shared the node's disk. The owner accepted on 2026-11-12 that restores are not queued (D20), and the cost of concurrent restores is part of the measurement owed under "Validation owed".

The gateway's `checkpointConcurrency` is unchanged: a replica still starts due attempts of different pods concurrently. The measurement owed compares pods of 2 and 4 slots that checkpoint together, with and without the queue, for each slot's pause and for the time until every slot holds a fresh snapshot.

### Best-effort capture

On a runtime that does not declare the `checkpoint` capability the adapter runs the same passes without the handshake and without the operation lock. The capture pass tolerates change instead of failing:

- A file whose identity changes during its read is read again, up to `bestEffortRereads` times, a constant of 2. A file still changing after that is stored as last read, and the manifest flags it `torn`.
- A file or directory that disappears between the walk and the read is left out of the snapshot.
- The attempt never fails because a file changed.
- New chunks stream into the packer as they are read. No pause holds the workspace still on this path, so nothing has to be held in memory or in the spool. The first harness version buffered the chunks of a best-effort capture in memory and was killed when a 4 GiB database file changed.

The snapshot is tagged `consistency: best-effort` and records the count of torn files.

On the measured nodes, under the harness's 512 MiB byte budget, a 4 GiB file under constant writes was over budget in every round on gVisor and Kata, where `FICLONE` is unavailable, and the attempt ended on this path with no pause and the file flagged `torn`. Under runc the same file was cloned during the pause in 2.0 to 2.5 s, and the snapshot stayed consistent. The AWS re-validation through the broker repeated both outcomes: gVisor and Kata `block-plain` ended on the best-effort path with the file flagged `torn`, and under runc the clone took 11.0 s and kept the snapshot consistent (see "Spool", **Clone time of a large file under writes.**). Under the share bound of "Capture", a 4 GiB file is larger than the session's spool share at the default cap, so it is now read in place on every runtime.

### Git repositories

The adapter treats a directory that contains a `.git` directory, or a `.git` file that names a git directory, as a repository.

- **Lock files.** Files named `*.lock` under a git directory are left out of every snapshot. A restored lock file blocks the next git command that needs the lock, and git treats a missing lock file as unlocked.
- **Temporary packfiles.** Files named `tmp_pack_*`, `tmp_idx_*`, and `tmp_rev_*` under `objects/pack/` are left out of every snapshot. Git writes them during a fetch or a repack and renames them into place when they are complete, so a captured copy is always incomplete. On LLVM a running `git repack -a -d` held a 374 to 411 MiB temporary git packfile. With the exclusion the pause was 0.03 to 0.07 s on every runtime. Without it, the capture on most gVisor and Kata variants stayed over budget and fell to best effort.
- **Order on the best-effort path.** The adapter captures `HEAD`, `refs/`, `packed-refs`, `index`, and the remaining metadata before `objects/`. Git writes objects before it updates the refs that point to them, so a ref captured earlier names objects that already existed when the adapter read `objects/`. Git removes objects only through gc, prune, or repack. When a git packfile listed earlier disappears during the read, the adapter lists `objects/pack/` once more.
- **Repacks.** `git gc` and `git repack` write new packfiles whose bytes differ from the old ones, so a repack causes the history to upload again. Under [decision D6](#decisions) the git configuration that `netagent` renders, which every git process in a slot reads as its system scope through `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM`, sets `gc.auto=0` and `maintenance.auto=false`, which [`0127-egress-and-git`, "Git configuration"](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#git-configuration) adopted as its decision 59, so no git command in a slot repository repacks on its own. The file is git's system scope, so a runtime that wants automatic maintenance overrides it in its global or repository configuration or with `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, and a runtime or an agent can run `git gc` by hand. Either costs only that session: its next checkpoint uploads the rewritten packfiles, and a runtime author who enables maintenance accepts that cost.
- **Alternates.** Objects reached through `objects/info/alternates` outside the slot are not captured. The warm repository stores of [`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Warm stores"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#warm-stores) are such objects. They are mounted read-only at `/lenny/git-stores` in every slot container ([PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout)), and a slot repository names a store's `objects` directory in its alternates as an absolute path under it. The adapter runs no git on slot repositories: it captures `objects/info/alternates` as a file, through the broker, and never follows it. The pod's `lenny-store-fill` init container fills the stores before READY, under a UID of its own ([WM-15](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-15-warm-up-checkout-and-reflink-rebuild), EG decision 61), for the entries on its pool's warm list, which are the `gitRepository` entries of the runtime's `sharedAssets`: an operator's public entries on a Runtime and a tenant's entries on a tenant-derived runtime. A store backs any `gitClone` source in the plan whose URL matches it, a client's source included, unless the source sets `depth` or `submodules: true`.
- **Capture copy.** Before a checkpoint's pre-copy pass, the adapter runs the helper's `capture-copy` subcommand on the plan's `gitClone` paths, as the slot UID through the supervisor's worker ([WM-10](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-10-git)). It copies into each repository's own object store the objects the repository needs and holds only through a store, leaving out `lenny.baseCommit` and its ancestors, the commit the repository was built at and its history. The snapshot therefore carries every object the session added beyond that commit and none of the commit's history, which a warm repository's history would make larger than the slot's `workspaceSizeLimitBytes`. Restore completion supplies that history on the restoring pod, from the pod's store or by a fetch of `lenny.baseCommit` ([`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Restore"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#warm-stores); [WM-10](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-10-git)). Pre-copy starts only after the run's `setupdone`, so the snapshot sees the copied objects. The worker runs asynchronously, so the supervisor keeps serving the broker during the run, and a long run, such as a repack of a large repository, no longer holds broker requests behind it. On AWS, before the worker became asynchronous, a 19.2 s checkout held a broker request for 19.1 s, and a capture copy of LLVM outlasted the adapter's 120 s broker deadline and made the slot dead ("Re-validation through the broker"). The run's deadline is inside the attempt's `checkpointAttemptTimeoutSeconds`, as `0153-workspace-materialization`, "WM-11" states.
- **Restore completion.** After a restore writes a workspace that holds plan repositories, the adapter runs the helper's `restore-complete` subcommand, which overwrites each repository's alternates with the pod's store for its recorded store URL, or removes them, and fetches what the pod's store lacks ([WM-10](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-10-git)). The restore writes the alternates file through the broker as it writes every other file, and `restore-complete` then replaces it.

### Change detection

An index entry is trusted when its identity matches the file's current identity and the entry is not racy. An entry is racy when the file's `mtime` falls within `racyWindow`, a constant of 2 s, before the instant the read that produced the entry started. This is git's rule for racily clean index entries. A write within the timestamp granularity of an earlier read leaves `mtime` unchanged, so a racy entry is re-read during the capture pass. `ctime` detects mode changes and renames onto the path, and the inode detects a replacement by rename. The rule holds on passed descriptors, whose `fstat` gives the same nanosecond times as `lstat`, and on the walk's `statx` results, which matched the slot's own `lstat` on every field for every entry on AWS.

Index identity is per mount view. Under gVisor's default mount the adapter's own view and the slot's view report different devices, 52 and 111 in the spike, so an index built from one view mismatches every entry in the other and costs a full re-read without an error. The index therefore holds only identities read in the slot's mount view, through broker-passed descriptors or relative to them, a restore included (IC-5).

### Change tracker

The stat walk is the largest part of the pause on gVisor and Kata, and its cost grows with the number of files in the workspace. The change tracker removes the walk from the pause by recording which paths changed since the last capture.

- **Mechanism.** The slot's supervisor holds the tracker's inotify instance. The adapter asks the broker for it with the `inotify` operation, which returns the shared descriptor, and adds a watch on every directory under the session's trees with the `watch` operation, 253 directories per request. The directory list comes from the preceding walk. `watch` pins each directory with an `O_PATH` descriptor and calls `inotify_add_watch` on it with the mask `IN_MODIFY | IN_ATTRIB | IN_CLOSE_WRITE | IN_CREATE | IN_DELETE | IN_MOVED_FROM | IN_MOVED_TO | IN_DELETE_SELF | IN_MOVE_SELF` and `IN_ONLYDIR`, and returns the watch descriptors as integers. The adapter reads the events from the shared descriptor as they arrive and keeps a set of dirty file paths and dirty directory paths. Each event also feeds the per-slot disk watch of [PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction), which keeps a running total of each tracked volume's use from them, and an overflow, the event cap, or a fallback to the walk also starts the watch's recalibration walk ([PL decision D12](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#decisions)). The tracker covers only the directories it watches: it sets no watch on the reserved names, on a directory that `checkpointExcludes` leaves out, on a directory recorded as `metadata_only`, or on a directory whose watch failed. The disk watch measures those directories by a walk at every disk-watch interval of 10 s, and while the tracker is degraded it measures the whole volume by the walk, as PL-5 states. A volume that a project quota bounds is the exception: the disk watch reads its use with `fstatfs` on the slot's root, so the tracker's events and those walks do not feed the watch for it, while the tracker keeps serving the checkpoint ([PL-10](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-10-project-quotas)). A checkpoint's walk does not replace the watch's recalibration walk, because it skips the reserved names and the `checkpointExcludes` subtrees, whose bytes the watch counts ([decision D23](#decisions)). A directory created later gets a watch when its `IN_CREATE` event arrives, and the adapter then lists it once, so files created before the watch existed still enter the dirty set.
- **Link groups.** inotify reports a write through one name of a hard-linked file under that name only. In the spike a write through a link in another directory reported the written name and missed the other name of the same inode. When a dirty path's identity shows a link count above 1, the capture marks dirty every index entry with the same device and inode. An inode whose link count exceeds the names the index holds for it has a name the tracker does not watch, such as one in a directory that `checkpointExcludes` leaves out, so every capture checks that inode's identity through a name the index holds.
- **Swap.** When the tracker starts, the adapter has the broker create the session's barrier file with `mkfile` in the session's workspace tree and keeps the writable descriptor it returns. The barrier file goes through the broker in every step: it is created there, and every write to it goes through the descriptor the broker returned. At the capture, the adapter writes one byte at offset 0 through that descriptor and asks the tracker to swap. The tracker reads events until it sees the barrier file's `IN_MODIFY`, returns the dirty set, and starts an empty one. Every event queued before the barrier write is therefore in the returned set. The write has to go through a descriptor the broker opened, because under gVisor an event reaches only the watches on the mount the change was made through. The barrier file is left out of every snapshot. A swap that sees no barrier event within its deadline falls back to the walk.
- **Event cap.** The adapter counts the events it reads between two swaps and treats the 16,384th as an overflow. Under runc and on both Kata file systems the kernel's queue holds 16,384 events and reports `IN_Q_OVERFLOW` when it fills: after a 20 s reader stall and 300,000 creates, the reader saw exactly 16,385 events under runc and on Kata `block-plain`, the queue's limit and the overflow event, and Kata's shared file system overflowed during the stall. gVisor exposes no inotify limits, and on both of its mounts its queue drained 600,000 events after a 20 s reader stall without an overflow. A stalled tracker on gVisor would hold every event in sentry memory, and the overflow fallback would never fire, so the adapter's cap stands in for the queue's bound there.
- **Fallback.** The capture walks in place of the swap when the tracker reports an `IN_Q_OVERFLOW` since the last swap, when the event cap was reached, when a `watch` has failed, when the tracker started after the previous snapshot, when the swap's barrier event did not arrive, or when the pre-copy cross-check found a change the tracker did not report. A failed watch and a failed cross-check mark the tracker degraded until the adapter restarts it, because a directory without a watch stays unwatched. A `watch` refused with `EACCES` on a directory the capture records as `metadata_only` does not degrade the tracker, because the capture holds none of that directory's entries. An overflow is sticky: every capture after it walks, until a walk completes and the adapter starts a fresh dirty set from that walk's result. On AWS the checkpoint after an overflow walked and missed no change, and so did the next one. `lenny_checkpoint_tracker_fallback_total` counts each fallback by reason.
- **Visibility.** The tracker's watches are on the slot container's own mount, which is the mount the slot's writes go through. In the spike that tracker reported 212 dirty paths for 200 files plus renames and the other operations under runc, with the barrier seen in 0.3 to 0.7 ms, and 211 under gVisor's default mount, where a tracker in the adapter container reported none. The adapter-held tracker of revision 12 saw writes from another container only under runc, gVisor's pod-share mount, and a Kata block volume (see "Kubernetes checks"). On AWS the supervisor-held tracker saw every change on every file system measured, runc on XFS, gVisor's default and pod-share mounts, Kata `block-plain`, and Kata's shared file system virtio-fs, in the steady, after-checkout, and package-install scenarios on Chromium, LLVM, and Linux: the verification walk found 0 missed changes and the pre-copy cross-check 0 unreported changes ("Re-validation through the broker"). The adapter decides whether a tracker runs from the file system type of the slot's mount, which it reads with `fstatfs` on the workspace broker root (see "Change tracker setting").
- **Limits.** The tracker needs one watch per directory. The measured workspaces needed 46,969 to 47,109 watches for Chromium and 17,153 to 17,198 for LLVM. A slot whose directory count exceeds `checkpointTrackerMaxWatches` (default 262,144) runs without a tracker.
- **runc.** Under runc the watches count against the node kernel's per-user limits, `fs.inotify.max_user_watches` and `fs.inotify.max_user_instances`, of the host UID that created the inotify instance, which is the slot UID as the pod's user namespace maps it. runc pools require user namespaces (`hostUsers: false`), which map each pod's slot UIDs to a host UID range of the pod's own ([PM-1](../0136-placement/change.md#pm-1-node-classes-and-presets), "User namespaces on the standard class"), so each pod's trackers have budgets of their own. Without user namespaces every pod used the same slot UID for the same slot index, and the trackers of one slot index on every pod of a node shared one budget: on AWS four pods with Chromium's 47,106 watches each held 188,424 watches in slot UID 20000, against the node's `max_user_watches` of 251,978, so a sixth would have exceeded it. The kernel sizes `max_user_watches` from memory, and `max_user_instances` defaults to 128, with one instance per tracker. A tracker whose watch fails falls back to the walk, so exhaustion costs pause length rather than correctness. Deployers raise both limits with a sysctl on runc nodes (see "Levers"). The budget under user namespaces is under "Validation owed". Under gVisor and Kata the limits belong to the sandbox or the guest kernel, so pods on a node do not share them.
- **gVisor.** Under gVisor's default mount, each container of the pod reaches an `emptyDir` through its own gofer mount, and the sentry delivers an inotify event only to watches on the mount the change was made through. The supervisor-held tracker watches the slot's own mount, so it sees the slot's writes on the default mount. The pod annotations `dev.gvisor.spec.mount.<volume>.share: pod`, `.type: bind`, `.options: rw`, and `.source: <volume>` make a volume one mount owned by the sentry and shared by the pod's containers. The sentry then keeps the volume's content in a single file, `.gvisor.filestore.<id>`, in the `emptyDir` directory on the node, the kubelet's size accounting counts that file, and events cross containers. The containerd runtime handler for gVisor has to pass `dev.gvisor.*` pod annotations through to runsc, which is a node setting. The pod controller renders the hint on every slot workspace and session volume and on the spool volume (IC-8). Under SI decision D8 a slot container mounts its whole volume: in the spike the hint on a volume mounted through `subPath` gave the adapter and the slot disjoint views, so the adapter would have checkpointed none of the slot's files and reported no error. The tracker no longer needs the hint to see events, and the hint gives no reflink, because `FICLONE` returns `EOPNOTSUPP` on both mounts. It stays for the sentry's descriptors (next bullet) and for throughput. On that mount the AWS stat walk was 3.2 times faster than on the default mount and Chromium restored in 56 s against 177 s, because the sentry writes one file on the node in place of 516,000 (see "Benchmarks"). Through the broker the parallel stat walk cost 3.1 to 3.4 µs per entry there against 9.8 to 10.2 µs on the default mount, and Chromium restored in 64 s against 235 s at the gp3 baseline ("Re-validation through the broker"). The content is page cache on the node, as on the default mount: a pod with a 1 GiB memory limit wrote 3 GiB into the volume without an out-of-memory (OOM) kill.
- **gVisor descriptors.** On the default mount, the sentry holds one host file descriptor for each watched directory, in addition to its cached directory entries, and the sandbox inherits containerd's `LimitNOFILE`, which is 65,535 on a default install. In single-container runs with Chromium's watches in place, the first walk exhausted that limit. File opens inside the sandbox then failed with `EMFILE`, for every process in it, and inotify events stopped arriving without an overflow event or an error. The adapter-held tracker never ran on the default mount, because the adapter saw `9p` there. The supervisor-held tracker can, and the AWS re-validation showed that it brings the hazard back. At containerd's `LimitNOFILE` of 65,535, tracker setup for Chromium's 47,106 watches succeeded, the sentry then held 65,530 to 65,535 host descriptors, and the next walk failed with `EMFILE` on 27,192 of 564,882 entries. The next checkpoint failed when the adapter could not open its own spool file, `kubectl exec` into either container then failed, and the pod could be removed only by force. The tracker reported no overflow and no error. At a `LimitNOFILE` of 1,048,576 the same sequence succeeded, with a peak of 96,422 host descriptors, about 2 per watched directory, a 0.041 s pause, and 0 missed changes, and LLVM's 17,198 and Linux's 6,355 watches ran at 65,535 without error. A pod with several slots runs every slot in one sandbox, so their watches add up. On the pod-share mount the sentry held at most 211 host descriptors in the re-validation and 777 in "Benchmarks", so the hint removes the hazard. [Decision D17](#decisions) keeps the tracker off on the default mount under `auto`.
- **Kata.** The guest kernel sizes `fs.inotify.max_user_watches` from guest memory, and a 2 GiB guest allows 15,514 watches. Chromium's watches failed with `ENOSPC`, and the tracker marked itself degraded and fell back to walks as specified. The Kata `kernel_params` setting `sysctl.fs.inotify.max_user_watches=1048576` removes the limit. On Kata's default shared file system, virtio-fs, a change made from one container produced no event in another container's watches. The supervisor-held tracker watches from the container that writes, and the slot's writes happen inside the guest kernel, so it sees them: on AWS it missed no change on virtio-fs in any scenario, and its steady pause was 0.094 s for Chromium. With `emptydir_mode = "block-plain"`, the volume is an `ext4` file system inside the guest, and the tracker's steady pause was 0.043 s. On Kata the tracker therefore runs on both file systems under `auto` ([decision D17](#decisions)). Its pauses during a package install stay long on virtio-fs, 12.65 s for Chromium, because each changed small file costs about 264 µs to read there.
- **Cost.** Through the broker on AWS, tracker setup for Chromium's 47,106 watches took 1.44 s under runc, 2.0 s on a Kata block volume, 6.4 s on gVisor's pod-share mount, 11.7 s on gVisor's default mount, and 12.9 s on Kata's shared file system. Most of it was the listing of the tree that finds the directories, which pre-copy's walk can supply, and adding the watches at 253 per request took 0.1 to 2.2 s. The adapter's reader cost 0.4 to 1.4 µs per event and kept up with 10,000 file creations per second. The adapter's memory is dominated by the file index, about 1.0 to 1.3 GiB with Chromium's index, and reached 2.0 GiB after a package install of 150,000 files, so the tracker's own share could not be separated. "Benchmarks" holds the earlier figures of the adapter-held tracker, and both runs give the effect on the runtime's own file operations.

The tracker is an optimization of the change source. Correctness rests on the fallback rules and on the racy rule, which apply to tracked paths in the same way as to walked ones.

## IC-2: Storage format

### Chunks

A file of at least 1 MiB is split with content-defined chunking (FastCDC with a gear hash), with a minimum of 256 KiB, an average of 1 MiB, and a maximum of 4 MiB. A smaller file is one chunk. A chunk's ID is the SHA-256 of its uncompressed bytes. Content-defined boundaries keep an insertion near the start of a large file from changing every later chunk.

### Packs

A pack is a sequence of compressed chunk frames followed by a footer that lists each chunk's ID, offset, compressed length, uncompressed length, and compression method. The target size is 16 MiB, which matches today's `DefaultChunkSizeBytes`. `packMaxBytes` is a constant of 32 MiB that the specification states, and no setting changes it. The gateway refuses a grant for a pack larger than `packMaxBytes` (see IC-3), because the bound in S5 depends on it. The footer also carries the pack nonce, 128 random bits that the adapter draws for every pack it builds, for checkpoints and exports alike. A pack's ID is the SHA-256 of its bytes, nonce included, so no two packs the adapter builds share a pack ID, and the gateway can refuse every key its catalog holds (S1) without blocking an upload. Deduplication works on chunks, which the nonce does not change, so a chunk already stored is referenced in the pack that holds it and never packed again to be reused ([A18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions)). Packing small chunks keeps the object count and the per-request cost down for workspaces with many small files.

### Manifests

A snapshot's manifest is a sorted stream of entries, one per path, with the path stored as a byte string. The manifest keeps the session's two trees apart: each entry names its tree, `workspace` or `session`, and its path relative to that tree's root, so that a download renders only the workspace tree and a restore writes both. Each entry records:

- the tree;
- the type: regular file, directory, symbolic link, hard link, FIFO, or socket;
- the mode, with every permission bit as captured;
- the size, and for a sparse file its data extents as pairs of offset and length, where `SEEK_DATA` and `SEEK_HOLE` on the passed descriptor report holes;
- `mtime` in nanoseconds;
- a symbolic link's target, as a byte string;
- for a hard link, the path of its link group's first entry in manifest order, which carries the content;
- the chunk ID list, which covers a sparse file's data extents in order;
- flags such as `torn` and `metadata_only`.

The manifest records no numeric owner. A slot UID depends on the slot index, and a restore can run in another slot or on another pod, so every restored entry belongs to the restoring slot's UID, which owns every entry a slot creates. The manifest records no `atime`, no `ctime`, and no extended attributes (IC-1, "Reading the slot's files"). Under gVisor `SEEK_DATA` reports a sparse file as one data extent, so a sparse file captured there is restored without holes. The format is versioned. The manifest stream is itself chunked with content-defined chunking and stored in packs, so an unchanged region of a large manifest deduplicates across snapshots.

The snapshot root lives in Postgres: the format version, the manifest's chunk list, the referenced pack IDs, the consistency tag, the parent snapshot, and counts of files, logical bytes, new bytes, and torn files.

### Object keys

A pack is stored at `/{tenant_id}/checkpoints/{session_id}/packs/{pack_id}`, where `session_id` is the session whose pod wrote the pack. The per-attempt `{checkpoint_id}` prefix is removed, because packs outlive the attempt that wrote them. A pack of a platform chunk set is stored at `/platform/chunk-sets/{source_id}/packs/{pack_id}` for a source the platform owns, and at `/{tenant_id}/chunk-sets/{source_id}/packs/{pack_id}` for a source a tenant owns (IC-4, "Platform chunk sets").

### Compression and encryption

Chunks are compressed with zstd from `github.com/klauspost/compress`, which `go.mod` already carries as an indirect dependency through `minio-go`. A chunk whose compressed size is at least 97% of its input is stored uncompressed, which skips packfiles and media. Encryption is unchanged: each `PUT` replays the signed server-side encryption headers that [§13.2.3 Checkpoint Capability Model](../../spec/13_security-model.md#1323-checkpoint-capability-model) defines per backend. A pack of a platform-owned chunk set is encrypted with the deployment key, and a pack of a tenant-owned set with the tenant's key when its tenant key state is `active` ([`0150-tenant-security-settings`, "TSS-4"](../0150-tenant-security-settings/change.md#tss-4-tenant-key)). A snapshot of a tenant whose `encryption.tenantKey` is `required` can therefore reference chunks of an operator's source that are stored under the deployment key, which the owner accepted on 2026-11-13.

## IC-3: Checkpoint stream

The `Checkpoint` stream in `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto` keeps its gateway-driven grant and confirm structure, and its messages change from tar chunks to packs.

- `CheckpointStart` adds `parent_snapshot_id`, `pack_target_bytes`, `pause_budget_ms`, `capture_budget_bytes`, and `precopy_rounds`, and the session's output-file chunk lists and leased packs, each leased pack with the chunk IDs its footer lists, so the dedup set of a pod that did not write them includes them. It drops `chunk_encoding`, which today names the tar chunks' encoding, `tar` or `tar.gz`.
- `CheckpointProbe`, the adapter's first frame, carries the size probe's result. It reports `workspace_bytes`, `file_count`, and `estimated_new_bytes`, which is the size of the files whose identity changed. The gateway reserves quota against the estimate.
- `PackReady` declares `pack_id`, `length`, and `content_md5`. The gateway answers `CheckpointAbort` without signing anything when the declared `length` is zero or larger than `packMaxBytes`, so the size a grant can carry is a gateway constant and the bound in S5 holds. It then resolves the pack ID against the session's own object key, `(tenant_id, owner_session_id, pack_id)`. When the catalog holds that key and the pack is in the session's allowed set, it answers `PackStored`, the "already stored" answer: the adapter skips that upload and references the pack, and the answer extends the lease of an `unreferenced` pack. That case is the adapter's resend of a pack it built, with the same nonce, after a `PackCommitted` the stream lost. When the catalog holds that key in any other state, a soft-deleted row included, the gateway refuses it, as S1 states, with `CheckpointAbort`. When the catalog holds no row for that key, the gateway consults the packs in the session's allowed set that another session owns, the packs of a derived session's starting snapshot, and answers `PackStored` for a match, for the "already stored" answer only. Any other pack is answered `PackGrant` with a single-key `PUT` grant that signs `Content-Length`, `Content-MD5`, and the encryption headers. The answers reveal nothing outside the session's own keys and its allowed set. Before it signs, the gateway applies §11.2's signing-time headroom check to the attempt or export the grant serves, and a refusal there is `CheckpointAbort` too. The adapter never rebuilds a pack to reuse it: a retry references the chunks of confirmed packs under lease through its dedup set, and chunks it uploads again go into new packs with new nonces, new pack IDs, and new grants.
- `PackCommitted` confirms an upload. The gateway checks the object's length with `StatObject` and inserts a pack row in state `unreferenced`, with a lease. The adapter adds the pack's chunks to its dedup set only at this confirmation, so a checkpoint and an export of the same session never reference a chunk whose pack upload is still in flight.
- `CaptureDone` reports `quiesced_ms`, `consistency`, `changed_files`, `torn_files`, and `rounds`.
- `SnapshotReady` carries the manifest chunk list, the referenced pack IDs, and the adapter's consumed message-id set in report order ([`0135-message-delivery`, "MD-3"](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-3-inbox-states-and-custody)), sent in batches for large snapshots. The gateway runs the reference closure check and writes the snapshot row and its pack references in one transaction. A closure failure answers `ReferenceRefused` with the offending pack IDs, and the adapter evicts their chunks from its dedup set, uploads the chunks again, and sends `SnapshotReady` again.
- `CheckpointSummary` and `CheckpointFailed` keep their meaning. Today `CheckpointSummary` closes a successful stream with the totals the gateway checks before it finalizes, and `CheckpointFailed` ends the stream when the object store rejects a `PUT`, carrying the store's HTTP status and error code.

On the runtime connection, `CH-MSGSOCK`, which carries the quiesce frames under RP O7 (a), `checkpoint_complete` changes meaning from "the snapshot is uploaded" to "the snapshot is captured". The runtime's 60-second autonomous resume stays as a safety net, and `deadlineMs` carries the pause budget. Today every periodic attempt sends `deadline_ms = 0`, because nothing sets the checkpointer's `Deadline` (see "Defects found while verifying").

The gateway's `SweepDue` runs due attempts concurrently, up to `checkpointConcurrency` per gateway replica, 16 by default. `checkpointConcurrency` is a gateway setting: the chart value `gateway.checkpointConcurrency` renders as the gateway's environment variable `LENNY_CHECKPOINT_CONCURRENCY`, which the gateway flag `--checkpoint-concurrency` reads, as `gateway.checkpointGrantWindow` renders as `LENNY_CHECKPOINT_GRANT_WINDOW`. Today it runs them one at a time, so one slow session delays every other session on the replica. The gateway carries grants and catalog writes, and the pack bytes travel between the pod and object storage.

### `ExportOutputFile` stream

The gateway opens an `ExportOutputFile` stream to the adapter for each output file, a `file` output part that names a workspace path, as [`0135-message-delivery`, "MD-18 Files"](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files) states. The stream sits beside `Checkpoint` in `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto` on its own channel, `CH-OUTPUTFILE`, which the pod fences by the session's coordination generation as it fences `CH-CHECKPOINT`. It follows the same gateway-driven grant and confirm structure, and reuses `PackReady`, `PackGrant`, `PackStored`, `PackCommitted`, and `ReferenceRefused` with the checks above. It adds these messages:

- `ExportStart`, the gateway's first frame, names the session, the part, the path, the effective limit, and `pack_target_bytes`. The gateway records the instant it sends it as `started_at` in the part's export record (IC-4, "Catalog"), and `exportTimeoutSeconds` counts from it.
- `ExportProbe`, the adapter's answer, carries the file's size and `estimated_new_bytes`, the bytes of the file's chunks that the dedup set does not hold. The gateway refuses a file over the effective limit, and it reserves quota against the estimate (IC-4, "Quota").
- `ExportReady` carries the file's chunk list with each chunk's offset and length in the file and its pack ID, the referenced pack IDs, and the SHA-256 of the whole file, which the gateway keeps as metadata (IC-5, "Download"). The gateway treats the list as untrusted. It checks that the offsets are contiguous from 0, that each chunk's length is at most the 4 MiB maximum chunk and matches the length the pack's footer lists for that chunk, and that the lengths sum to the size `ExportProbe` reported, which is within the effective limit, so a missing or overlapping chunk is refused. It runs the reference closure check, answers `ReferenceRefused` on a failure as for `SnapshotReady`, and writes the `output_files` row, its `output_file_packs` references, and the export record's state `ready` in one transaction.
- `ExportAbort`, from the gateway, ends a failing export in place of `CheckpointAbort` and carries the reason: `too_large`, `quota_exceeded`, `pack_empty` for a declared length of zero, `pack_too_large` for one over `packMaxBytes`, `pack_key_held` for a key S1 refuses, `export_timeout`, `seal_timeout`, or `pod_evicted`. The adapter removes the clone and closes the stream. A `PostAgentOutput` `REJECT` comes after `ExportReady` has closed the stream, so its reason, `rejected`, reaches the runtime only in `output_failed` ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)).

The adapter opens the file through the slot's file-descriptor broker when it reads the part, whether the export starts at once or waits in the export queue. It translates the part's path, which is a path in the slot container, to a path beneath the matching broker root, and the broker's resolution refuses a path that leaves the root or names a link. A part's `path` names a file under `/workspace`, and the adapter translates it to the `workspace` broker root ([PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout)). It freezes the file as MD-18 states, into `/adapter/spool/<n>/export/{adapter_id}` on the spool volume, when the file fits the remaining export share of the spool (IC-7, "Spool cap"): with a reflink clone from the passed descriptor where the reflink probe found reflink mode for the slot's volume, and with a `copy_file_range` copy where it found copy mode, counted at full size ([decision D18](#decisions)). A file larger than the remaining export share, and every file on a slot volume in read mode, is read through the passed descriptor in place with MD-18's identity check, made with `fstat` on that descriptor. On AWS the copy of a 256 MiB file took 0.056 s on gVisor's pod-share mount, and an in-place export of a 1 GiB file that the runtime rewrote during the read failed with `file_changed` on gVisor and on Kata `block-plain`. It holds at most `files.maxQueuedExports` plus `files.maxConcurrentExports` open export descriptors per session, each a descriptor the broker passed, and its count is authoritative: it relays a part beyond the count with `unopened: true`, and the gateway refuses exactly the marked parts with `too_many_exports`. It closes a part's descriptor and removes its clone when the part's export ends, and when it receives `output_failed` for the part, whether or not a stream was ever opened for it. It then chunks and packs the file as IC-2 states, and checks each chunk against the dedup set when it packs the chunk, so a chunk a checkpoint confirmed while the export ran is referenced rather than uploaded. The export takes no quiesce and no pod-wide operation lock. During a checkpoint's capture pass, the adapter pauses export reading, hashing, and compression, so an export does not compete with the capture for disk and CPU while a quiescing runtime is quiesced, and uploads of packs the export already built continue ([A17](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions)).

An export ends at `ExportReady`, at a failure, or at `exportTimeoutSeconds` (900 seconds by default) from its `ExportStart`, after which the gateway fails the part, releases the export's unused reservation, and sends `ExportAbort`, and the adapter removes the clone. A part waiting in the export queue fails `exportTimeoutSeconds` after its first frame. At session end the seal checkpoint runs first, the gateway signs no export grant while it runs, and exports still running finish in the time it leaves in the session's seal window before they fail with `seal_timeout` (IC-5, "Seal").
