On a preStop drain of the agent pod, the eviction checkpoint runs first, the gateway signs no export grant while it runs, exports continue only with the budget the checkpoint leaves, and an export stream never uses the stream-drain stage of [§10.1.7](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1017-prestop-hook-drain) (IC-7, "Exports on a drain"). A new coordinator after a handoff takes over the session's export records, reopens the stream for each part still exporting without reserving again, and the adapter reuses its clone and confirmed packs. A stream that the adapter cancels when it acknowledges the new coordinator's fence is not a failure for the previous coordinator, which stops it without releasing the reservation, changing the export record, or sending `output_failed` (IC-4, "Quota"). MD-18, "Export lifecycle", states these cases and the `output_failed` notice the runtime receives.

The gateway counts a session's outstanding pack grants across its `Checkpoint` and `ExportOutputFile` streams against one window, the pool's effective `checkpointGrantWindow`, on the session's coordinating replica, so the bound in S5 holds per session. After a handoff, the new coordinator counts the whole window as outstanding until the grant TTL has passed since the pod acknowledged its fence, and the adapter cancels the previous generation's open `Checkpoint` and `ExportOutputFile` streams at that acknowledgement (S5). A large export therefore slows the pre-copy and upload passes of a concurrent checkpoint of the same session. It does not lengthen the pause, because the capture pass uploads nothing and the export's own reading waits during it.

## IC-4: Catalog, reference closure, and quota

### Catalog

| Table | Columns |
| :-- | :-- |
| `checkpoint_packs` | `tenant_id`, `owner_session_id`, `pack_id`, which together are the key, `bytes`, `state` (`unreferenced` or `referenced`), `lease_expires_at`, `created_at`, `deleted_at` |
| `checkpoint_snapshots` | `snapshot_id`, `tenant_id`, `session_id`, `parent_snapshot_id`, `trigger`, `consistency`, `manifest_chunks`, `consumed_message_ids` (the adapter's consumed message-id set in report order), counts, `created_at`, `deleted_at` |
| `checkpoint_snapshot_packs` | `snapshot_id`, `owner_session_id`, `pack_id` |
| `output_files` | `output_file_id`, `tenant_id`, `session_id`, `part_id`, `path`, `name`, `mime_type`, `size_bytes`, `sha256` of the whole file, kept as metadata, `chunks` (each chunk's ID, offset and length in the file, pack ID, and offset within the pack), `blob_ref` (the file's `lenny-blob://` URI), `expires_at` (the session's retention), `created_at`, `deleted_at` |
| `output_file_packs` | `output_file_id`, `owner_session_id`, `pack_id` |
| `platform_sources` | `source_id`, `owner` (`platform` or a tenant ID), `kind` (`store` or `assets`), `source_key` (the store's normalized URL, or the runtime's name), `latest_contribution_id`, `created_at`, `retired_at` (set when the source leaves every warm list and runtime) |
| `platform_packs` | `source_id` and `pack_id`, which together are the key, `bytes`, `contribution_id`, `created_at`, `deleted_at` |
| `platform_contributions` | `contribution_id`, `source_id`, `pod_name`, `staged_at` (the commit a store's checkout staged, or the runtime revision of the assets, kept to seed file indexes and never read for eligibility), `manifest_chunks`, `created_at` |
| `checkpoint_snapshot_platform_packs` and `output_file_platform_packs` | `snapshot_id` or `output_file_id`, `source_id`, `pack_id` |
| `output_file_exports` | `session_id` and `part_id`, which together are the key, `tenant_id`, `state` (`queued`, `exporting`, `ready`, or `failed`), `first_frame_at`, `started_at`, `reserved_bytes`, `confirmed_bytes`, `reservation_released_at`, `coordination_generation` (the generation of the coordinator that drives the export), `failure_reason`, `notice_acked_at` |

A pack is named by its object key, `(tenant_id, owner_session_id, pack_id)`, because the pack nonce keeps an honest adapter's pack IDs distinct but a pod chooses the bytes it declares, so two sessions can still write packs with identical bytes, and every reference table names the owner with the pack ID. A pack row moves from `unreferenced` to `referenced` when a snapshot or an output file first references it, in the transaction that writes that reference. It never moves back. `state` records that a pack was referenced once, and liveness is the query over the reference tables of IC-6, "Pack reclamation", rather than the state. A `referenced` pack whose last snapshot has rotated away and whose last output file is gone therefore leaves the allowed set at once, because no source of the allowed set names it, its chunks leave the dedup set when `CheckpointStart` or `Resume` next lists the session's packs, and a reference to it that an attempt in flight still makes is refused with `ReferenceRefused`, after which the adapter uploads the chunks again in new packs. Garbage collection deletes it once its lease has expired. An `unreferenced` pack keeps its lease for `packLeaseSeconds`, a gateway setting, 1800 seconds by default, which is twice the longer of the `checkpointAttemptTimeoutSeconds` and `exportTimeoutSeconds` defaults. Pool admission refuses a pool unless `packLeaseSeconds` is at least twice the larger of its `checkpointAttemptTimeoutSeconds` and `exportTimeoutSeconds`, so a retry that runs a whole deadline after a timed-out attempt or export still finds the packs that attempt confirmed. Each `SnapshotReady`, `ExportReady`, and "already stored" answer that references a pack extends its lease to `packLeaseSeconds` from that moment.

The gateway writes an `output_file_exports` row, the export record, when it reads a file part from the session's stream, with `first_frame_at`, and sets `started_at` when it sends `ExportStart` ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files), "Clocks"). The row carries the export's quota reservation (IC-4, "Quota"), and each `PackCommitted` adds the pack's bytes to `confirmed_bytes` in the transaction that inserts the pack row. A new coordinator reads the session's rows after the pod acknowledges its fence, writes its own generation to each row whose reservation is unreleased, with an update guarded by `coordination_generation < $gen`, which takes the reservation over without a second one, reopens `ExportOutputFile` for each `exporting` row, keeps each `queued` row waiting, and applies `exportTimeoutSeconds` from `started_at` and from `first_frame_at`. A `failed` row keeps the failure notice until `notice_acked_at` is set, and the gateway sends the notice to the runtime again after a resume. The rows live as long as the session's other records.

The checkpoint records the adapter's consumed message-id set in report order in `consumed_message_ids` ([`0135-message-delivery`, "MD-3"](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-3-inbox-states-and-custody)). On a resume, the coordinating replica reads the set of the snapshot the session resumes from, and moves every message the gateway recorded as consumed after the newest recorded id in it to `consumed_unrecorded`. Custody of a message ends at consumption, so no consumed message waits for a checkpoint.

### Reference closure

A session's allowed set is the union of these packs:

- packs that the session's own retained snapshots reference;
- packs that the session's live output files reference;
- packs that the snapshot the session started from references, for a derived session, its platform packs included;
- `unreferenced` packs that the session owns with an unexpired lease; and
- the packs of the platform chunk sets of the session's eligible sources, which the gateway computes at each checkpoint and export (see "Platform chunk sets" below).

The gateway accepts a snapshot only when its referenced pack IDs are all in the allowed set. The allowed set leaves out the source session's later snapshots, so a session derived from a live source cannot read content the source wrote after the derive. The gateway runs the same check on an output file's referenced pack IDs before it writes the `output_files` row. The reference closure transaction locks the `checkpoint_packs` rows it references with `FOR SHARE`, so garbage collection cannot soft-delete a pack between the check and the write of the reference. A failure answers `ReferenceRefused` with the offending pack IDs.

The adapter builds its dedup set from the same sources, using the manifest it holds locally, the chunk lists of the session's output files, and the leased packs that `CheckpointStart` and `Resume` name, and it uses that one dedup set for checkpoints and exports. A chunk enters the set only after its pack is confirmed, and a `ReferenceRefused` evicts the named packs' chunks, so the set does not keep a pack that expired or was reclaimed. The gateway answers two existence questions. The first is the "already stored" answer to `PackReady`. It resolves the pack ID against the session's own keys first and against the allowed set's packs that another session owns only for that answer, so the answer reveals nothing beyond the session's own keys and its allowed set. The second is whether one of the session's eligible sources holds a chunk, which "Platform chunk sets" states, and whose answer covers only content the session may already read. The adapter never asks whether a chunk exists anywhere else.

The manifest's chunk-to-pack mapping is untrusted input. It can name only bytes inside packs the gateway authorized for the snapshot, and S4 detects a mapping that names the wrong bytes.

### Platform chunk sets

[Decision D1](#decisions) keeps the allowed set per session and adds one exception for content the platform itself placed on its pods. A file of a session matches a chunk of a platform chunk set by content alone, and the session references that chunk in place of uploading it.

**Sources and owners.** A platform source is one of these:

- A warm repository store ([`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Warm stores"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#warm-stores)), identified by its owner and its normalized URL. Its set holds the working trees of the warm-up checkouts of [WM-15](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-15-warm-up-checkout-and-reflink-rebuild), without `.git`. The capture copy leaves the base commit's history out of every snapshot (IC-1, "Git repositories"), so a set needs no git object.
- A runtime's shared assets of type `artifact` and `inline`, which the adapter writes to `/lenny/assets` before READY ([PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout)). Under decision D25 of [`0140-runtime-revisions`, "RV-3"](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md#rv-3-pods), an assets source belongs to a platform runtime, keyed by the base revision its pod set's `PodSet` names, or to a derived runtime whose pod set carries a `sharedAssets` append, keyed by that pod set's key. A derived runtime is platform-derived, one a `platform-admin` registers, or tenant-derived, one whose `inline` and `artifact` assets a tenant admin writes for its owned pool ([`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Tenant shared assets"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#tenant-shared-assets)). A derived runtime's pods hold the base's assets and the appended ones on one volume, so its pods contribute the base's files to the base's source and the appended files to the derived runtime's own source.

The platform owns the sets of the sources an operator declares: the public `gitRepository` entries on a Runtime's `sharedAssets` and the assets source of every platform runtime and platform-derived runtime. A tenant owns the sets of the sources its tenant-derived runtimes declare: the stores of their `gitRepository` assets, an asset that a `platform-admin` writes on the runtime included, because that store serves the owner alone and holds a repository private to the tenant, and the assets source of their `inline` and `artifact` entries ([`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Tenant shared assets"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#tenant-shared-assets)). A `gitRepository` entry without `prestage: true` adds no warm-up checkout ([WM-15](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-15-warm-up-checkout-and-reflink-rebuild)), so no staged tree feeds a set from it, and its store is no chunk source. One URL that both an operator and a tenant declare is two sources. A platform pack holds the chunks of one source only, so a pack that a session may read through one source exposes nothing of another.

**Who adds chunks.** Only a never-launched pod, one to which no session has bound on any slot (SIR-38), adds chunks to a set, with one exception **[design]**: the upload route of a tenant-derived runtime's `artifact` asset chunks the uploaded bytes under IC-2 into packs of that runtime's tenant-owned assets set, and those packs are the upload's only stored copy, so a pod's later contribution of the file finds every chunk present and uploads none ([`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, "Tenant shared assets"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#tenant-shared-assets)). An upload records no contribution, so its chunks become eligible for a session only through a pod's contribution that lists them. The contribution is step 6 of the start-up order in [PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction), after the warm-up checkouts are staged and before READY:

1. At warm-up, after the stores are filled, the warm-up checkouts are staged, and the assets are written, and before the pod reports READY, the adapter reads each source's content: one slot's staged tree `.lenny-prestage/<entry>` through that slot's file-descriptor broker, leaving out `.git`, and the asset files under `/adapter/assets`. It never reads a pristine tree or anything else in the store volume, which holds repository data only `lenny-store-fill` parses ([WM-9](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-9-archive-extraction-leaves-the-gateway)). No session has bound to the pod, so no session code, setup commands included, has run there, and the adapter trusts the broker's replies (S6, SIR-38).
2. It chunks each file under IC-2 and builds packs of one source each, leaving out every chunk the source's set already holds, which it learns from the gateway's answer for that source. A staged tree's chunks go to the source of the warm-list entry that staged it: a tenant-derived runtime's `gitRepository` asset feeds the tenant-owned set of that URL, whoever wrote it, and an operator's public entry the platform-owned set. When such an asset and an operator's public entry share one store, every tree staged from that store feeds the tenant-owned set, whichever entry carries `prestage: true`, so a tenant entry never feeds a platform-owned set, even when an operator declares the same URL. The answer is safe to give, because no session code has run on the pod (S6).
3. It uploads the packs on `CH-SOURCECHUNKS`, a pod-level stream that carries IC-3's `PackReady`, grant, and `PackCommitted` messages and a final `ContributionReady` with the contribution's manifest. The gateway signs a grant only to a pod whose record shows no bind, and only for a source on that pod's warm list or its runtime's assets.
4. The gateway records the contribution, with the commit or asset revision it staged, and records against the pod which contribution describes each source's content on it.

A contribution's manifest lists every file of the staged content with its chunk IDs, whether or not the pod uploaded them. A pod whose staged commit or asset revision already has a contribution uploads nothing and is recorded against that contribution; a pod whose staged content has none records one, uploading only the chunks the set lacks. A new commit of a store adds the chunks of the files that changed. The contribution delays only the contributing pod's READY, and only for chunks no earlier pod added. A pod to which a session has bound, a reused pod included, adds nothing. A tenant-owned source receives contributions only while its tenant's `securitySettings.storage.platformChunkReuse` is `true` ([`0150-tenant-security-settings`, "TSS-1"](../0150-tenant-security-settings/change.md#tss-1-settings-model)). Two pods that contribute at once each record a contribution, and the second leaves at most one pod's worth of duplicate chunks, which garbage collection reclaims when no reference holds them.

**Eligibility.** At every checkpoint and every export, the gateway computes the session's eligible sources from what the session may read **[design]**:

- the stores in the session's `coveredStores`, which holds the stores whose read checks the session passed and the stores of public platform entries; and
- the assets sources of the session's runtime: its own for a platform runtime, and for a derived runtime with a `sharedAssets` append both its own and its base's, which for a tenant-derived runtime are a tenant-owned source and a platform-owned one.

A withheld store is never eligible, because its repository is one the session's user cannot read. The adapter's word never decides eligibility. A tenant whose `platformChunkReuse` is `false` has no eligible source. A session is eligible for a source's content only as its own pod holds it: for a store, the contribution of the commit that the pod's store held at READY, and for assets, the contribution of the asset revision the session's runtime revision mounts. Older commits of a store and other revisions of the runtime's assets are never eligible, so content removed from a repository's history or from a runtime's assets stops matching for every session whose pod no longer holds it. `CheckpointStart`, `ExportStart`, and `Resume` name the eligible contributions.

**Matching.** The adapter adds the chunk lists of the session's eligible contributions to its dedup set. A chunk of any session file whose ID is in that set is referenced rather than uploaded, whatever the file's path and however it reached the workspace: a staged checkout, a checkout of another commit, a file the agent copied or moved, or an asset the runtime copied. IC-2 makes a file under 1 MiB one chunk named by the file's SHA-256 and cuts a larger file at boundaries its content sets, so two pods that check out the same commit with the pinned git and the rendered git configuration produce the same chunk IDs, and so does the reflink rebuild, which copies the same bytes. A file that differs in any byte matches nothing, which costs an upload and never a wrong restore. The gateway answers no question about a set on behalf of a session, so a session can match only the chunks its eligible contributions list, which is content its pod holds in `/lenny/git-stores` and `/lenny/assets`.

**Adoption.** When a session adopts a staged tree at bind (WM-15), the adapter seeds its file index for the adopted files from the contribution whose staged commit equals the staged tree's commit, keyed by the identity the adapter recorded when the tree was staged, as it seeds the index after a restore (IC-5, "Restore"). The adoption renames the tree to the path of the first `gitClone` source of its repository within the slot's workspace volume, so each file keeps its inode number and modification time, and the recorded identity still describes it. No process runs in the slot between staging and bind ([SI-3](../0145-session-isolation/change.md#si-3-runtime-processes-slot-release-and-setup-commands)), so an unchanged identity means unchanged content. The first pre-copy then reads no adopted file the session left unchanged. When no contribution matches the staged commit, the first pre-copy reads the files and references whatever chunks the sets hold.

**Restore and derive.** A snapshot's references to platform packs are part of the snapshot. A restore fetches them under S3, and a derive gives the derived session the platform packs its starting snapshot references, as it gives the source session's own packs, because the derived session receives those files either way.

**What a session can learn.** No session writes to a set, and a session can match only content it can already read. The probes that defeat sharing between sessions find nothing here:

| Threat | Outcome |
| :-- | :-- |
| A probe: the session writes a guessed file and watches whether it uploads, through the pod's byte counters in `/proc/net/dev`, which every container of a pod shares under runc, gVisor, and Kata, or through checkpoint timing or the quota | The outcome depends only on the platform content of the session's eligible sources, which the session can read, so it reveals nothing about any other session. |
| A compromised adapter names a chunk it does not hold | It can reference only chunks of its eligible sources, whose content the session can already read through its own mounts, `/lenny/git-stores` and `/lenny/assets`. |
| Poisoning: wrong bytes under a chunk ID | Sessions cannot add to a set. S4 still verifies every chunk at restore. |
| Reading a whole pack | A platform pack holds one source's chunks, so a borrowed chunk exposes nothing outside a source the session may read. |

**Residual risks.** The documentation states these:

- A set keeps every chunk any contribution added, but a session matches only the contributions of the content its pod holds, so earlier commits and earlier asset revisions never match.
- A user who loses read access to a repository keeps the snapshots that reference that store's packs, as the same files sat in the snapshot before this decision.
- A pod compromised before its first bind can add packs whose bytes do not match their chunk IDs. S4 then fails the restores of every session that references them, a denial of service across sessions of the eligible sources rather than a disclosure.
- Removing content from a set early, for example a secret committed to a warm-list repository, deletes packs that snapshots still reference, which fails their restores. Pack reclamation never does this, so an operator who must purge such content deletes the referencing snapshots or accepts that their restores fail (IC-6).

The adapter holds the chunk lists of the contributions for its pod's staged content once per pod for all its slots. For a Chromium checkout of about 565,000 files the list holds a similar number of 32-byte IDs, which "Validation owed" measures.

### Quota

The tenant is charged once for each pack, at confirmation, and the charge is released when the pack is deleted. A checkpoint therefore charges for its new bytes only. Each confirmed pack of a platform chunk set has one `artifact_store` row under its set's owner, with `artifact_type` `platform_chunks`: a pack of a platform-owned set is recorded under the platform tenant, the tenant ID `platform` that §10.2 reserves, so the operator pays for it and no customer's quota counts it, and a pack of a tenant-owned set is charged to its tenant once, at confirmation. [`0121-billing-resource-attribution`](../0121-billing-resource-attribution/change.md) bills from those rows. A snapshot that references platform packs adds nothing to its tenant's counter for them (IC-4, "Platform chunk sets"). Today every checkpoint charges for the whole workspace. The reservation at the start of an attempt uses `estimated_new_bytes`. An export on the `ExportOutputFile` stream reserves the `estimated_new_bytes` of its `ExportProbe`, or is refused with `quota_exceeded`. Each new pack it writes is charged once at confirmation, and the charge draws down the export's reservation rather than adding to it. The estimate is the pod's report, so the gateway also applies §11.2's signing-time headroom check per export: it refuses to sign a grant whose length would carry the export's granted total past its reservation plus the tenant's remaining headroom, and a pod that reports zero new bytes still cannot exceed the quota. The export record holds the reservation under the rules §11.2 states for a checkpoint's: the atomic Redis increment records `reserved_bytes`, and every party that ends the export, the success and failure arms, a new coordinator, and the [§12.5](../../spec/12_storage-architecture.md#125-artifact-store) backstop for a row of a terminal session, releases it with one guarded `UPDATE ... SET reservation_released_at = now() WHERE session_id = $1 AND part_id = $2 AND coordination_generation = $3 AND reservation_released_at IS NULL`, applying the decrement of `reserved_bytes − confirmed_bytes` only when that update changed the row. Every release and every state transition of an export record carries `AND coordination_generation = $gen`, the writing coordinator's own generation, or for the backstop the generation it read, so a coordinator that a handoff replaced changes no record and releases no reservation. A stream that a fence acknowledgement cancels is not a failure for the previous coordinator: it stops without releasing the reservation, changing the record, or sending `output_failed`. `Rehydrate` adds `SUM(reserved_bytes − confirmed_bytes)` over the `output_file_exports` rows whose `reservation_released_at` is null, beside the checkpoint reservations, so a rebuilt counter holds every outstanding export reservation and the later guarded release stays correct. Every terminal outcome settles the reservation exactly once. Writing the `output_files` row settles it, and a failure, an `exportTimeoutSeconds` expiry, a `seal_timeout`, a `PostAgentOutput` `REJECT`, and a pod loss release what the confirmed packs did not draw down. A session runs at most `files.maxConcurrentExports` exports at once, 2 by default, and at most `files.maxQueuedExports` parts, 8 by default, wait for that cap, and a waiting part holds no reservation ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)).

## IC-5: Restore, derive, download, and seal

### Restore

The gateway selects the snapshot: the latest finalized snapshot on resume, or the starting snapshot at a derived session's first bind, when the session is first attached to a pod. It signs `GET` grants for that snapshot's packs, the platform packs it references included (IC-4, "Platform chunk sets"), as the restore requests them, which keeps each grant's 30-second TTL meaningful for large restores. `Resume` also carries the session's output-file chunk lists and its leased packs, each leased pack with the chunk IDs its footer lists, so the restored pod's dedup set covers them (IC-4, "Reference closure").

The adapter fetches the manifest chunks first, then the packs, with `restoreConcurrency` requests in flight, 8 by default. `restoreConcurrency` is an adapter setting: the chart value `controller.adapterRestoreConcurrency` renders as the controller flag `--adapter-restore-concurrency`, and the controller passes it as the adapter flag `--restore-concurrency` to every agent pod it creates. It decompresses each chunk, verifies its SHA-256 (S4), and writes each of the session's trees through the slot's file-descriptor broker, from the `workspace` and `session` broker roots, in this order:

1. `mkdir` creates each directory with mode 0700, so the supervisor can create the directory's entries whatever its final mode.
2. `mkfile` creates each regular file with `O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_EXCL` and its recorded mode, as the slot UID, and returns a writable descriptor, through which the adapter writes the file's data extents and sets its size. `mkfifo` creates each FIFO, `symlink` each symbolic link with its recorded target, and `link` each member of a link group after the group's first entry.
3. `utimens` sets each entry's `mtime` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW`, deepest entries first, so that setting an entry does not change a directory already set.
4. `chmod` sets each directory's recorded mode.

No path may escape the root through `..` or an absolute path, which the broker's resolution refuses, and setuid and setgid bits are dropped, which the broker's mode mask enforces. A symbolic link is restored with its recorded target, whatever it names, because no operation of this design follows a link (IC-1, "Reading the slot's files"), so `ExtractTree`'s rule that restored a link only when its target resolved inside the root goes. Restored entries belong to the slot UID with their recorded modes, so the slot can `chmod` and touch them, and the rule of `0145-session-isolation` revision 2 that widened restored modes for the adapter is retired (SI decision D9). In the spike, owner, mode (0000, 0400, 0500, and 0300 included), nanosecond `mtime`, hard links, sparse extents, FIFOs, and symbolic links came back exactly for 23 of 23 entries, where a restore through the adapter's own mount got 22 of 23 wrong. On AWS, broker restores of Chromium and LLVM with that coverage set added, 564,902 and 208,064 entries, came back with 0 differences under runc and on gVisor's pod-share mount. On Kata `block-plain` with a dedicated `fsGroup` the only differences were the set-group-ID bits of 4 directories, which the broker's mode mask drops by design, so a restored directory there stops passing the `fsGroup` group to new files. A `metadata_only` entry is restored with its mode and `mtime` and no content, a socket is not restored, and the adapter reports both to the session.

The adapter then builds the file index with an identity pass, the stat walk of IC-1, after the `utimens` and `chmod` steps, which change each entry's `ctime`, so the first pre-copy after a restore reads nothing. On AWS the first pre-copy after every broker restore read 0 files. The restore limit of each tree is its volume's size limit, `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` for `/workspace` and `sessionSizeLimitBytes` for `/session`, in place of the fixed 2 GiB.

When the restored workspace holds plan repositories, the adapter then runs the helper's `restore-complete` (IC-1, "Git repositories"), inside the `Resume` or bind call's deadline. The helper applies the plan's sources only after the seed, as [`0153-workspace-materialization`, "WM-13"](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md) states, and a restore runs no source and no setup command of its own.

Restore time still grows with workspace size. On AWS a restore through the broker took 1.0 to 1.2 times the time of the same restore code writing through the adapter's own mount on the same node, for Chromium and LLVM, under runc, on gVisor's two mounts, and on Kata `block-plain`: for example 210 s against 205 s for Chromium under runc at the gp3 baseline, and 33.0 s against 27.1 s on gVisor's pod-share mount at 16,000 IOPS. The local spike's ratio of 1.8 to 2.2 for 10,000 files of 4 KiB did not reproduce at that scale, because the disk or gVisor's gofer bounds the restore and the broker's one request per 253 files hides behind it ("Re-validation through the broker"). Lazy restore, which fetches a file on first access, is out of scope.

### Derive

A derive writes a snapshot row for the new session that references the source snapshot's manifest and packs, its platform packs included, and it copies no object. The new snapshot becomes the derived session's starting snapshot, which places its packs in the derived session's allowed set. A derive copies no output file of the source, and the derived session's allowed set leaves out the packs that only the source's output files reference. [`0144-session-derive`](../0144-session-derive/change.md) keeps its modes: a derive with `from: latest` uses this reference, and one with `from: start` rebuilds from the plan as that design states. [Decision D2](#decisions) records the alternative of copying packs. A derive reads no workspace file, and the derived session's first bind restores its starting snapshot, both trees, through the broker as a resume does, then runs `restore-complete`.

A derive with `from: latest` therefore adds no bytes to the tenant's storage counter, because the tenant was charged for each referenced pack when the source confirmed it (IC-4, "Quota"). The storage reservation that session-derive takes ahead of the first copy, and its undo through `undoDerivedWrites`, cover only the uploads a derive with `from: start` copies. Under an effective `checkpointPolicy` of `none` (IC-8), the source has no snapshot, and a derive with `from: latest` is refused with `422 DERIVE_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE` and `details.reason: checkpointing_disabled`. Both are requests to session-derive (see "Changes requested of other designs").

### Download

`GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` stays a gzip-compressed tar stream. The gateway renders it from the manifest and the packs, using its own object-store credentials, and streams it to the client. The tar holds the workspace tree only, without the reserved names and without a `lost+found` directory at its root (IC-1, "Reading the slot's files"). The `/session` tree, which holds the runtime's private state, is left out of the download, of every output file, and of a derive with `from: start` ([PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout)). The manifest is untrusted input from the pod, so the gateway bounds it before it renders: the sizes of the manifest's workspace entries sum to at most the pool's `workspaceSizeLimitBytes`, and a manifest over that total fails the download. The download reads no pod file, so the broker does not affect it. The tar carries each entry's mode and `mtime`, a hard-link entry for each member of a link group after the first, and a FIFO entry for each FIFO. It leaves out sockets, carries an `metadata_only` entry with its mode and no content, and names no numeric owner.

The gateway renders an output file from its chunk list and packs the same way. It accepts a `Range` header, which the row's chunk offsets in the file and offsets within the pack resolve to byte ranges of the packs that hold the range, and it verifies the SHA-256 of every chunk it serves (S4). S4's per-chunk check is the storage-integrity guarantee for an output file, and the `ExportReady` checks of IC-3 catch missing or overlapping chunks. The whole-file SHA-256 that `ExportReady` carried is metadata in the `output_files` row. The gateway publishes it as `sha256` on the released file part and in a `Repr-Digest` header (RFC 9530) on each response of `GET /v1/blobs/{ref}`, which describes the full file in a `206` response too, so a client can verify its download end to end. The gateway compares it only where it already reads the whole file before release, the `PostAgentOutput` scanner's read, and refuses the part on a mismatch as a `REJECT`. The hash cannot detect a compromised pod, which writes both the chunk list and the hash, and the tier 1 tests and the adapter conformance tests catch an adapter that orders chunks wrongly ([A20](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions)). [`0135-message-delivery`, "MD-18 Files"](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files) states the route.

### Seal

The seal runs the same passes with trigger `seal`, through the slot's file-descriptor broker, and the resulting snapshot becomes `final_workspace_ref`. The seal and the session's exports read the slot's files, so the slot release runs its Sweep and removes the session's trees only after the seal checkpoint and the session's exports have ended, and the slot's supervisor serves the broker until then (see "Changes requested of other designs"). The seal is a checkpoint attempt, so it waits in the pod's checkpoint queue while another slot's attempt runs (IC-1, "Staggering across slots"), and that wait counts against its seal window. The seal checkpoint runs first, within the session's seal window, which keeps its meaning as the retry window for object-store failures of the seal ([§7.1](../../spec/07_session-lifecycle.md#71-normal-flow), **Seal-and-export invariant:**). The gateway signs no export grant while it runs, so the seal has the session's whole grant window. The seal captures the workspace, which holds every exported file, and it shares one dedup set with the exports, so an exported file's chunks upload once, and an export that continues after the seal references the chunks the seal confirmed. Exports of the session still running, or waiting in the export queue, when the seal checkpoint ends finish in the time it leaves in the same window, and under an effective `checkpointPolicy` of `none`, which takes no seal snapshot, they get the whole window. Each part still exporting or waiting when the window ends fails with `seal_timeout`. §7.1's terminal pipeline budget stays the seal window plus the release allowance of 30 s ([A19](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions)). A file the runtime changed or deleted after emitting its part is exported from its clone, whose chunks the seal does not hold, and an export pack whose grant is outstanding when the seal starts is not yet confirmed, so the seal may upload its chunks again, at most one grant window of packs. The seal window is the runtime revision's `maxWorkspaceSealDurationSeconds` when set, and otherwise the pool's, at most the chart value `gateway.workspaceSealMaxDurationCeilingSeconds`, as MD-18, "Seal window" states.

## IC-6: Retention, garbage collection, erasure, and legal hold

- **Retention.** The latest 2 snapshots per session are retained, as today, and legal hold suspends rotation. Output files follow the session's retention rather than snapshot rotation, and a legal hold on the session keeps them ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)). A pack that any live snapshot or output file references stays, so an output file can keep a pack alive after every snapshot that shared it has rotated.
- **Pack reclamation.** Garbage collection deletes a pack when no live snapshot and no live output file references it and its lease has expired. It computes liveness with a query over `checkpoint_snapshot_packs` and `output_file_packs` instead of a stored counter, so the count cannot drift, and a pack that a rotated snapshot shared with an output file stays. Before it soft-deletes a pack, it locks the pack row with `FOR UPDATE` under `deleted_at IS NULL` and re-checks liveness and the lease in that transaction, so a reference closure transaction that holds the row `FOR SHARE` either commits its reference first or finds the pack deleted and refuses the reference (IC-4, "Reference closure"). The deletion path keeps the rules of [§12.5 Artifact Store](../../spec/12_storage-architecture.md#125-artifact-store): soft delete first, guarded quota release, then the object delete, with the backstop sweep. The guarded quota release returns the tenant's quota only when the soft delete changed the row, so two concurrent deleters cannot release it twice. A soft-deleted pack row keeps its key for `gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds` while the object delete or the backstop sweep is still pending, so S1 refuses a grant for that key and never revives the row: a revived upload could be deleted after the fact. The pack nonce keeps an honest adapter from ever declaring such a key. The backstop sweep is a periodic garbage collection pass that deletes objects a failed primary cleanup left behind.
- **Compaction.** When the adapter builds its dedup set, it treats chunks in packs that the parent snapshot uses below `packRewriteThreshold`, a constant of 0.5, as absent. Their live chunks go into new packs, and the sparse pack loses its last reference after rotation, or at the end of the session's retention when an output file still references it. Compaction needs no gateway knowledge of chunks.
- **Erasure.** `DeleteByUser` deletes the user's snapshots and output files, and `DeleteByTenant` deletes the tenant's. A pack that another user's derived snapshot references stays until that snapshot goes. This matches today's outcome, where the derive copy survives the source user's erasure.
- **Security holds.** A security hold under [FE-3](../0128-forensics-evidence-preservation/change.md#fe-3-platform-only-holds-and-a-security-retention-class) that includes `checkpoints` copies each held snapshot to escrow. Under this design the copy of a snapshot is every pack its manifest references, the manifest's own packs included, written to the hold's escrow prefix, with the snapshot root recorded among the hold's items. The copy references no pack outside that prefix. Pack reclamation and compaction therefore cannot remove escrowed content, and a pack shared with another snapshot of the session or with a derived session is copied whole. A hold whose `include` lists `artifacts` covers the output files of a held session ([A16](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions)), and it copies each one as it copies a held snapshot: every pack the file's chunk list references goes to the hold's escrow prefix, and the `output_files` row is recorded among the hold's items. Snapshot rotation, the deletion of an output file at the end of the session's retention, the deletion of an `output_files` row after a `PostAgentOutput` `REJECT`, which includes the refusal of a part whose scanned bytes do not match its whole-file SHA-256, pack reclamation, `DeleteByUser`, and `DeleteByTenant` call `EnsureCopied` before they soft-delete a snapshot row, an `output_files` row, or a pack row, and a copy failure refuses the deletion, which the deleter retries, as FE-3 states for every deletion path.
- **Legal-hold escrow.** The §12.8 legal-hold escrow, which migrates a held tenant's resources to the escrow bucket on a forced tenant deletion, handles a pack-backed item, a snapshot or an output file, the same way: it copies every pack the item references, the manifest's own packs included for a snapshot, and it records the item's row.
- **Platform chunk sets.** A platform pack is live while any snapshot or output file references it, or while it holds a chunk of a contribution that a live pod is recorded against, or of the source's most recent contribution while the source is not retired. Garbage collection computes that with a query over `checkpoint_snapshot_platform_packs`, `output_file_platform_packs`, and the latest contribution, and deletes a pack that none of them holds, under the same soft delete, `FOR UPDATE` re-check, and backstop sweep as a session's pack. A source retires when it leaves every warm list and every runtime, and from then its packs live by reference alone. Erasure: a platform-owned set holds operator content rather than user data, so `DeleteByUser` does not touch it, and the user's snapshots release their references when they go. `DeleteByTenant` deletes the tenant-owned sets with the tenant's other objects, and destroying the tenant key makes the packs stored under it unreadable. Holds: a security hold's escrow copy and the §12.8 legal-hold escrow copy every platform pack a held snapshot or output file references, as they copy its other packs, and the deletion of a platform pack calls `EnsureCopied` first. Purge: pack reclamation never deletes a referenced platform pack. An operator who must remove content from a set, such as a secret committed to a warm-list repository, deletes the snapshots that reference it, or deletes the packs and accepts that those snapshots no longer restore. The operator guide states this.
- **Partial manifests.** The tar-prefix reassembly in [§10.1.7 preStop Hook Drain](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1017-prestop-hook-drain) **Reassembly on resume.** is removed ([decision D4](#decisions)). A timed-out attempt leaves confirmed packs that the next attempt reuses, and a resume uses the latest finalized snapshot.

## IC-7: Size limits and drain budgets

- **Workspace limit.** The pool's `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` keeps its name and now bounds disk use and storage. [EG-2](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#steps) renders `emptyDir.sizeLimit` on the workspace volume, which the tree omits today, because the egress design relies on the limit. Under [PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction) each slot has its own workspace and session volumes and the spool sits on a volume of its own, so IC-7 sets the checkpoint's part of the values the pod controller renders: each slot's workspace volume takes `workspaceSizeLimitBytes`, each slot's session volume takes `sessionSizeLimitBytes`, which PL owns, and the spool volume takes the value under "Spool cap". Whichever of EG-2 and IC-7 lands second keeps the other's rendering and applies its own part. The adapter's size probe and the restore use each tree's limit.
- **Default and rendering ([decision D3](#decisions)).** The default `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` is 10 GiB in place of 512 MiB, so that a session can clone a large repository, which the pause no longer forbids. The pod controller renders each disk-backed volume's `sizeLimit` from its setting, as [PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction) and its PLR-12 state, and an `ephemeral-storage` request on the pod equal to the sum of those `sizeLimit` values plus the resource class's own `requests.ephemeral-storage`, 1 GiB in the shipped classes and configurable per class, which covers the containers' writable layers and logs ([decision D21](#decisions)), so that the scheduler reserves the disk the volumes and the containers may use. Today the controller renders neither the workspace volume's `sizeLimit` nor any `ephemeral-storage` request on an agent pod. At the defaults a pod with one slot requests 10 GiB for `/workspace`, 1 GiB for `/session` ([PL decision D27](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#decisions)), 1 GiB for `/tmp`, 16 GiB for the spool, 1 GiB for the shipped class's own request, and the pool's `warmCloneBytes` for the stores, which is 29 GiB before the stores, and each further slot adds 12 GiB. The ResourceQuota defaults of [§17.8.2](../../spec/17_deployment-topology.md#1782-capacity-tier-reference) assume about 10 GiB per pod (2Ti for 200 pods, 25Ti for 2,500, and 150Ti for 15,000), so `requests.ephemeral-storage` becomes the tier's `pods` floor times the per-pod request of a one-slot pod at the defaults, about 5.7Ti, 70.8Ti, and 424.8Ti, rounded up to a whole Ti for tier 1 and to a multiple of 10Ti for tiers 2 and 3: 6Ti, 80Ti, and 430Ti for tiers 1 to 3, with `pods × warmCloneBytes` added by an operator whose pools keep warm stores. The tiered drain caps of §10.1.7, whose last tier ends at 512 MiB, are replaced under "Drain caps" below. The disk watch that fails a slot at `adapter.diskWatchThresholdPercent` of a volume's `sizeLimit`, 90% by default, stays as PL-5 states it.
- **Spool cap.** The pool's `checkpointCaptureBudgetBytes` caps one session's use of the spool volume: its spool under `/adapter/spool/<n>/checkpoint/`, its file index, and the output-file export clones that the adapter writes under `/adapter/spool/<n>/export/{adapter_id}` ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)). The adapter reserves each slot's cap within the spool's usable share, so one slot's staging or checkpoint never takes another slot's cap. Upload staging under `/adapter/spool/<n>/staging/` sits outside the cap, in the staging space the slots share ([`0153-workspace-materialization`, "WM-4"](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#wm-4-the-plan-the-adapter-builds), O9). Each session's checkpoint therefore behaves as it does on a pod with one slot. The adapter counts each file it places on the spool volume at its logical size, or at its allocated size, `st_blocks` times 512 bytes, when that is larger, and never by `statfs`. The kubelet counts every reflink clone at its full size against the volume's `sizeLimit`, although a clone adds almost no disk: in the spike three clones of 200 MiB into a spool volume with `sizeLimit: 300Mi` added about no disk, the kubelet counted 600 MiB, and it evicted the pod with "Usage of EmptyDir volume "spool" exceeds the limit "300Mi"". An eviction for one `emptyDir` ends every slot's session. `statfs` reports the whole node filesystem under runc and one volume only on gVisor's pod-share mount, so it measures neither the cap nor the limit. A copy in copy mode counts at its full size in the same way, and so do the spooled chunks, the file index, and the reflink probe's files.
- **Single clones.** A clone or a copy of one file into the spool is bounded by the session's remaining share of the cap, in reflink mode as in copy mode. A larger file is read in place and chunked (IC-1, "Capture"). On AWS a reflink clone of a changed 4 GiB file without that bound evicted the pod.
- **Spool headroom.** The spool volume's `sizeLimit` is the pool's `spoolSizeLimitBytes`, 16 GiB by default, one value for the pod whatever its slot count, which [`0153-workspace-materialization`](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md#open-decisions) settles as its O9. The adapter uses at most the usable share, `spoolSizeLimitBytes` divided by `spoolHeadroomFactor`, a constant of 2, which is 8 GiB at the default. It reserves each slot's spool cap out of the usable share and gives the remainder to upload staging, which the slots share: at the defaults a pod with one slot has 7.5 GiB of staging space and a pod with four slots 6 GiB. Pool admission refuses a pool whose usable share does not hold `maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod` times `checkpointCaptureBudgetBytes` plus the staging floor that WM-4 states. The adapter's own count stops each slot at its cap and staging at the shared space, and the half of `sizeLimit` the adapter never uses keeps the kubelet's count below the limit when the kubelet counts more than the adapter. On AWS a spool filled to exactly its `sizeLimit` was evicted, a fill to 75% of it stayed under the limit under runc, on gVisor's pod-share mount, and on Kata `block-plain`, and a fill to 75% was evicted on gVisor's default mount, where the kubelet's count of a volume has run at about 1.5 times the bytes written ("Kubernetes checks"). The factor of 2 covers that ratio, and "Validation owed" confirms it on gVisor's default mount. The volume's `sizeLimit` counts toward the pod's ephemeral-storage request ([PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction)). Export clones may use at most `exportSpoolFraction` of the cap, a constant of 0.5, so a checkpoint's capture pass, which keeps a quiescing runtime quiesced, always has at least half of the spool. The adapter clones a part when it reads it, so the clones of parts waiting in the export queue count against that share as well as those of running exports. On a slot volume in reflink or copy mode, a file larger than the remaining export share, 256 MiB at the default `checkpointCaptureBudgetBytes` of 512 MiB, is read in place with the identity check of MD-18 and is neither cloned nor copied ([A21](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#decisions), [decision D18](#decisions)). The adapter removes a clone when its export ends, whatever the outcome.
- **Attempt deadline.** The 60-second `CheckpointTimeout` splits into the pause budget and the pool's `checkpointAttemptTimeoutSeconds` (default 300) for the whole attempt. The first checkpoint of a session holding a large clone can span several attempts, and each attempt continues from the packs the previous ones confirmed.
- **Exports on a drain.** On a preStop drain of the agent pod the eviction checkpoint runs first, within `evictionCheckpointCapSeconds`, and the gateway signs no export grant while it runs, so the checkpoint has the session's whole grant window. Exports continue only with the budget the checkpoint leaves in the drain's checkpoint stage, and an export still running when that stage ends fails with `pod_evicted`, as does each part still waiting in the export queue. An export stream never uses the stream-drain stage of [§10.1.7](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1017-prestop-hook-drain), whose 30 seconds the grace floor reserves for the session streams, and a gateway replica's own preStop drain does not wait for its export streams, which the session's next coordinator reopens ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)).
- **Drain caps.** The tier table in [§10.1.7](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1017-prestop-hook-drain) sizes the preStop wait by total workspace bytes: it caps the wait at 30, 60, or 90 seconds according to the session's last measured workspace size (see "preStop drain and tiered drain cap" under "Terms"). Under this design a drain checkpoint uploads the bytes changed since the last snapshot, and the table's last tier ends at 512 MiB, below the 10 GiB default of decision D3, so the table becomes one pool setting, `evictionCheckpointCapSeconds` (default 90), and the gateway no longer reads `last_checkpoint_workspace_bytes` to choose a tier. The pool-config admission webhook's agent-pod grace floor keeps its structure with that single cap in place of `max_tiered_checkpoint_cap`: a pod's grace period covers a capped checkpoint for every session slot plus the stream drain. A pod's slots checkpoint one at a time (IC-1, "Staggering across slots"), so every wait for a pod's checkpoints scales with its slot count in the same way. The gateway waits for a pod's `CheckpointBarrierAck` up to `maxConcurrentSessionsPerPod × evictionCheckpointCapSeconds`, and the BarrierAck floor checks that `checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` is at least that product. A gateway replica's preStop wait for the checkpoints in progress of the sessions it coordinates allows the same product for each pod, and the gateway pod's grace budget of §10.1.7 is computed from it (see "Pool-config admission webhook and its floors" under "Terms").
- **Barrier hold.** The CheckpointBarrier of [§10.1.8 Rolling Updates and the CheckpointBarrier Protocol](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1018-rolling-updates-and-the-checkpointbarrier-protocol) holds tool-call dispatch until the capture pass ends instead of until the upload ends.

## IC-8: Checkpoint policy, interval, and change tracker

Today the only checkpoint setting is the gateway-wide interval, `--checkpoint-interval`, which the chart sets from `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`. A value of 0 falls back to the 600-second default (`interval` in `pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointer/checkpointer.go`), so no setting turns checkpointing off. Neither a Runtime nor a pool can set a policy or an interval.

### Policy

The Runtime definition ([§5.1 Runtime](../../spec/05_runtime-registry-and-pool-model.md#51-runtime)) and the pool configuration ([§5.2 Pool Configuration and Execution Modes](../../spec/05_runtime-registry-and-pool-model.md#52-pool-configuration-and-execution-modes)) each take an optional `checkpointPolicy`:

| Value | Checkpoints during the session | Seal at session end | Effect on the rest of the platform |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `full` (default) | Periodic, pre-scale-down, drain, CheckpointBarrier, and eviction checkpoints run as IC-1 states. | Runs. | Unchanged. |
| `sealOnly` | None. | Runs, and its snapshot becomes `final_workspace_ref`. | A pod failure or an eviction resumes the session with `resumeMode: conversation_only` and `workspaceLost: true`, from the [§4.4.5](../../spec/04_system-components.md#445-checkpoint-storage-failure-and-eviction-fallback) minimal-state record. The session never becomes `dormant`, because no checkpoint would restore its workspace: it stays `idle` on its pod, and when `maxClientIdleSeconds` passes with no activity it ends as `expired` with the reason `max_idle_time`, after the seal ([`0146-session-lifecycle`, "SL-6"](../0146-session-lifecycle/change.md#sl-6-idle-pods-and-dormancy), owner decision of 2026-11-13). A suspended session keeps its pod, and the release of a suspended session's pod after `maxSuspendedPodHoldSeconds` is not taken. Under `full`, a session suspended by an interrupt releases its pod after `maxSuspendedPodHoldSeconds` (900 seconds by default), and an idle session becomes `dormant`, which checkpoints the workspace and releases the pod at once. The session later resumes on another pod from that checkpoint. `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` and a derive with `from: latest` read the sealed snapshot once the session ends. |
| `none` | None. | Does not run. | Everything under `sealOnly`, the idle timeout ending the session included, and the session never has a workspace snapshot. `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` returns `404 RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND`. A derive with `from: latest` returns `422 DERIVE_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE` with `details.reason: checkpointing_disabled`, while a derive with `from: start` still rebuilds from the plan. |

The effective policy is the stricter of the two values, in the order `full`, `sealOnly`, and `none`, where an unset value counts as `full`. The runtime author knows whether the runtime's state lives in workspace files, and the operator owns the pool's storage budget, so either one can turn checkpointing down and neither can turn it back up. A runtime that [`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md) derives from a base inherits the base's policy and can only make it stricter.

The policy has these further effects:

- **Freshness.** Sessions whose effective policy is `sealOnly` or `none` are left out of `lenny_checkpoint_stale_sessions` and the `CheckpointStale` alert.
- **Visibility.** The session envelope reports the effective `checkpointPolicy`, so a client knows before a failure whether the workspace survives one.
- **Drain.** The preStop path and the [§10.1.8](../../spec/10_gateway-internals.md#1018-rolling-updates-and-the-checkpointbarrier-protocol) CheckpointBarrier skip the checkpoint step for these sessions, and the pool-config admission webhook's agent-pod grace floor leaves out the checkpoint cap for a pool whose effective policy is `none`.

The policy is separate from a runtime's ability to quiesce. A runtime that does not declare the `checkpoint` capability still gets checkpoints under `checkpointPolicy: full`, captured on the best-effort path without a `checkpoint_request`. Today the adapter sends `checkpoint_request` to such a runtime anyway, because the Checkpoint handler never checks the runtime's declared support (see "Defects found while verifying").

### Interval

The Runtime definition and the pool configuration each take an optional `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`. The effective interval for a session is resolved in this order:

1. the pool's value, when set;
2. otherwise the Runtime's value, when set;
3. otherwise the gateway default (`periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`, 600 seconds).

The gateway clamps the result to the deployer's bounds, `checkpoint.minIntervalSeconds` (default 60) and `checkpoint.maxIntervalSeconds` (default 3600). The pool's value wins over the Runtime's because the operator pays for the pool's object storage and its gateway load, and the deployer's bounds protect the gateway and the object store from any single pool. The owner settled this order on 2026-11-13 ([decision D9](#decisions)).

A tenant may also set `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds` on a runtime that [`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md) derives from a base, at or above the base's interval, which is the interval the order above gives a session on the owned pool with no tenant value. A tenant write with a lower value is refused, and a longer interval is accepted, so a tenant cannot raise the object storage and gateway load that the operator or the runtime author chose. The field is live, so the floor reads the base's current value at write and at resolve, and a session's interval is the longer of the tenant's value and the base's. A later base change that raises the base's interval above the tenant's applies at the next resolve, as that design's "DR-6: Base changes" states for live restrict-only fields. The deployer's bounds still clamp the result.

The gateway's schedule then carries a per-session interval. The first-checkpoint jitter applies the gateway's `periodicCheckpointJitterFraction` to the session's own effective interval. The jitter delays a session's first periodic checkpoint by a random share of the interval, up to that fraction (0.2 by default), so that sessions started together do not checkpoint together ([§4.4.4](../../spec/04_system-components.md#444-checkpoint-slos-and-scheduling)). The freshness service-level objective (SLO) and `CheckpointStale` measure each session against its own effective interval. An interval has no effect under an effective `checkpointPolicy` of `sealOnly` or `none`.

A short interval is cheap under IC-1, because each checkpoint uploads only the bytes changed since the last one. Under the current implementation, a 60-second interval on a 1 GiB workspace would pause a quiescing runtime for about 27 seconds of every minute, at the archive rates in "Measurements" and a 100 MiB/s upload. The interval override therefore lands together with IC-1 or after it, and never before it.

### Change tracker setting

The pool configuration takes an optional `checkpointChangeTracker` with the values `auto` (default) and `off`. Under `auto` the adapter runs the tracker when the file system type of the slot's mount, which it reads with `fstatfs` on the workspace broker root, is one on which the supervisor-held tracker is known to see every change: a node file system under runc, `ext4` on a Kata block volume, `virtiofs` on Kata's shared file system, and the sentry's `tmpfs` under gVisor's pod-share mount hint. The AWS re-validation measured each of them with 0 missed changes. It leaves the tracker off on `9p`, gVisor's default mount, under [decision D17](#decisions), because at Chromium's directory count the sentry's host descriptors reach containerd's default `LimitNOFILE` and every process in the sandbox then fails to open files, with no signal the adapter can act on. It also leaves the tracker off on any other `fuse` file system and on any type it does not recognize. No value forces the tracker on, because on a file system where it could miss changes without reporting the loss, or take the sandbox down, it would break R11. `checkpointChangeTracker: off` keeps every capture on the stat walk.

The pod controller renders the gVisor mount hint annotations for every slot workspace volume, every slot session volume, and the spool volume, `slot-<n>-workspace`, `slot-<n>-session`, and `lenny-spool`, on every pod of a pool with `isolationProfile: sandboxed` ([PL-5](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-5-volumes-size-limits-and-eviction)). Under SI decision D8 no slot container mounts its volume through `subPath`, which is the case in which the hint gave the adapter and the slot disjoint views. The hint lets the tracker run under `auto`, removes the sentry's descriptor hazard, and lowers the per-entry costs of the walk and the reads (IC-1, "Change tracker"). It takes effect on nodes whose containerd runtime handler passes `dev.gvisor.*` annotations to runsc, and it is ignored elsewhere, where the slot's mount reports `9p`, the capture walks, and the default mount's per-file costs apply. The adapter reports the effective value and the tracker's state in `lenny_checkpoint_tracker_state`.

### Runtime fields and revisions

[`0140-runtime-revisions`](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md) sorts a Runtime's fields into versioned and live classes ([RV-1](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md#rv-1-revisions)). The Runtime fields this design adds fall into these classes:

- `checkpointPolicy` and `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds` are live. A write to either applies to the next session on every revision in the runtime's traffic and creates no revision, and the resolution rules under "Policy" and "Interval" read the current value.
- `checkpointExcludes` ([D5](#decisions)) is versioned, because it names the regenerable directories the binary writes, and gateway-read under decision D14 of [`0140-runtime-revisions`](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md#decisions), because it reaches the pod only as session data. A write to it creates a revision that shares the pod set of the revision it amends, and a session's checkpoints leave out the list of the revision chosen for it under [RV-2](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md#rv-2-selection).
- **Delivery.** At the post-chain point the gateway composes the session's list, the chosen base revision's list with a tenant's appended entries, and stores it on the session record. It sends the list to the adapter on the call that assigns the session to the pod, `FinalizeWorkspace` or `Resume`. The adapter keeps it with the session's slot, leaves the listed directories out of every capture of that session, the seal included, and adds no tracker watch under them. A resume sends the stored list, including a resume that moves revision under D7 of that design, which keeps the session's stored plan as well (D19), so the list changes only for a new session.
- A tenant's `checkpointExcludes` on a derived runtime merges over the selected base revision: the session's list is the base revision's list with the tenant's entries appended. A tenant's `checkpointPolicy` on a derived runtime can only make the base's current policy stricter, as "Policy" states, and a tenant's `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds` can only lengthen the base's current interval, as "Interval" states.

## IC-9: Removal of the embedded `SIGSTOP` checkpoint path

[§4.4.3](../../spec/04_system-components.md#443-checkpoint-quiescence-timeout-and-abort-handling) lets an embedded adapter stop the agent with `SIGSTOP` for a checkpoint and resume it with `SIGCONT`, under a 60-second watchdog. The watchdog, or a failed check that the agent left the stopped state, sets `checkpointStuck`, and in embedded mode `/healthz` then fails the liveness probe so that Kubernetes restarts the pod. The owner has ruled out a mechanism that forces a runtime to pause (see "Owner's direction"), and the tree calls the path from nowhere (see "Current state"). IC-9 removes it ([decision D15](#decisions)):

- The **Embedded adapter mode only:** bullet of §4.4.3, the embedded adapter path under **Checkpoint timeout and recovery:**, **SIGCONT confirmation.**, and **Embedded adapter liveness integration:** are removed, and so are the `checkpointStuck` flag and `watchdogTimeoutSeconds`. The sentence of **Checkpoint abort cleanup:** that forbids recovering a checkpoint goroutine's panic while `SIGCONT` is unconfirmed loses its subject and is removed with them.
- Every runtime is checkpointed by one rule. It gets a consistent snapshot when it declares `checkpoint` and takes part in the quiesce handshake, and a best-effort snapshot on the path under IC-1, "Best-effort capture", otherwise. The platform never stops a runtime process for a checkpoint. The embedded deployment model leaves under [PL-2](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-2-removal-of-the-embedded-deployment-model), so no adapter and agent share a process, and every adapter reaches a slot's files through that slot's supervisor and its broker, as IC-1, "Reading the slot's files" states. The first-party runtimes that embedded the adapter, `echo` among them, become runtimes that declare a `command` and run in a slot container.
- `pkg/adapter/embeddedcheckpoint` is deleted. The `embedded` value of `pkg/checkpoint`'s `Level`, which maps to `consistent` only because of the `SIGSTOP` path, is removed, so the consistency of a checkpoint follows from the capture that produced it.
- The statements that rest on the path change with it: the embedded adapter sentence and the `SIGSTOP` clause of **Hard workspace size limit.** in §4.4.4, the "embedded-adapter pools" scope of `CheckpointDurationHigh` in §4.4.4 and §16.5, the `SIGSTOP` rationale of **Interrupt during checkpoint:** in §4.7.2, and the `SIGSTOP`/`SIGCONT` deliverable of §18 (see "Specification sections affected").
- Agent pods render no liveness probe today, and no rule in the specification asks for one once **Embedded adapter liveness integration:** is gone. [FE-1](../0128-forensics-evidence-preservation/change.md#fe-1-quarantine) relies on that, because a node-side freeze of a runtime container would otherwise fail a probe on `/healthz` and have the kubelet kill the frozen container.

IC-9 depends on no other IC proposal, and it can land first. It lands together with PL-2's removal of the embedded model or before it.

## Measurements

These numbers were measured on a laptop on 2026-11-05, before the cloud runs. They come from a Docker Desktop VM with no CPU limit and warm page caches. "Benchmarks" holds the cloud measurements, which supersede these where the two overlap.

| Measurement | Environment | Result |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Current archive path (a copy of `ArchiveTree`), this repository's `.git` | macOS, Apple M3 Pro, one goroutine, output discarded | 719 MiB in 8.1 s, 89 MiB/s |
| Same, this repository's `pkg/` | Same | 32 MiB in 0.92 s, 35 MiB/s |
| Same, 256 MiB of random bytes | Same | 2.8 s, 93 MiB/s |
| Same, a file appended to during the archive | Same | `archive/tar: write too long`, attempt fails |
| Same, a file created and removed during the archive | Same | `lstat ...: no such file or directory`, attempt fails |
| Same, a file rewritten during the archive | Same | No error in one run |
| Stat-only walk, 420,000 files in 70,000 directories | Linux arm64 container, 12 CPUs, overlayfs over ext4 | Sequential 2.12 s, 4 workers 0.51 s, 16 workers 0.35 s |
| Same | XFS loopback with `reflink=1` | Sequential 0.62 s, 4 workers 0.16 s, 16 workers 0.22 s |
| Stat-only walk, 388,091 files | macOS APFS, sequential | 8.9 s |
| `FICLONE` of a 2 GiB file | XFS loopback with `reflink=1` | 1.1 ms |
| `FICLONE` of 10,000 files of 4 KiB, against a plain copy | Same | 603 ms against 140 ms |
| SHA-256 and MD5 throughput in Go | Apple M3 Pro | 2,615 MiB/s and 718 MiB/s |
| Local copy of 512 MiB with `fsync` | macOS SSD | 1,379 MiB/s |

A parallel stat walk on Linux costs 0.4 to 0.8 µs per file, so a direct walk alone fits the R1 target at 500,000 files. Today's archive rate of 35 to 93 MiB/s, before any upload, is what makes a whole-workspace pause long. Under SI decision D9 no walk reads the workspace through the adapter's own mount. The parallel stat walk of IC-1, "The walk" brought the walk's own cost back to the direct walk's on AWS, 0.53 to 0.56 µs per entry under runc, yet Chromium's 565,000 entries paused 1.29 s, because the comparison of every entry against the index cost about 1 s in the harness. The walk alone therefore does not meet R1, and R1 holds with the tracker, whose pause was 0.035 s ("Re-validation through the broker").

## Session isolation spikes

The spikes used the layout of `0145-session-isolation` revision 3, so the paths in this section, such as `/workspace/slots` and `/workspace/warm-clones`, are the paths they ran with. [PL-3](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md#pl-3-runtime-visible-filesystem-layout) replaces them with `/workspace`, `/session`, and `/lenny/git-stores`.

Three local spikes on 2026-11-12 checked this design against the layout of [`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md). They ran on a kind v1.35.0 cluster or under Docker, with containerd 2.2.0, runc 1.2.9, gVisor `release-20261109.0` on the systrap platform, kernel 6.12.76-linuxkit for arm64 on an Apple laptop, and XFS on a loop device with `reflink=1` for the runc volumes. The broker spike gave gVisor an `emptyDir` without reflink. No cloud ran. The spikes' logs lived in a session scratchpad that does not persist, so this section holds every result this design relies on.

**Spike 1: compatibility with SI revision 2.** Every mechanism of this design worked through SI revision 2's per-slot layout under runc and gVisor: the identity comparison, the racy rule, `FICLONE` into the spool, export clones, the tracker under runc, confined reads, restore with that revision's mode rule, and cleanup. The spike refused every planted symbolic link, and a naive open would have read the other slot's secret and the adapter's environment. A change set was captured in 8.7 ms with 1.6 MiB added on XFS, against about 60 ms with 101.6 MiB added by the copy fallback. It found that the gVisor pod-share hint on a volume a slot mounts through `subPath` gives the adapter and the slot disjoint views with no error, which SI decision D8 removes, that the kubelet counts reflink clones at their full size, and that index identity is per mount view.

**Spike 2: SI decision D8's layout.** Slot containers mounted their whole volume at `/workspace/slots`. The adapter mounted both slot volumes and the adapter-only spool volume, and the warm clones were read-only at `/workspace/warm-clones` everywhere. The worker's `/var/lib/kubelet/pods` sat on an 8 GiB XFS loop file system with `reflink=1`, in a Restricted namespace with no `fsGroup`. The pods were `nr` under runc, `ng` under gVisor without hints, `nh` under gVisor with the pod-share hint on both slot workspace volumes and the spool volume, and `oh`, SI revision 2's `subPath` layout with the hint, as a negative control. The layout lost no file data on any runtime.

| Check | runc | gVisor | gVisor, pod-share |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `FICLONE` from a slot volume to the spool volume | 36 to 98 µs for 200 MiB | `EOPNOTSUPP` | `EOPNOTSUPP` |
| `copy_file_range` | 34 µs, extents shared | Full copy, 41 ms per 200 MiB | Full copy, 48 ms per 200 MiB |
| `read` and `write` | 127 ms, 200 MiB added | 105 ms | 111 ms |
| Sharing after the clone | FIEMAP: 209,715,200 of 209,715,200 bytes shared, `df` unchanged | FIEMAP `ENOTTY`, `df` +200 MiB per copy | FIEMAP `ENOTTY`, per-volume `statfs` +200 MiB per copy |
| Overwrite of 8 MiB in the original after the clone | Copy unchanged, `df` +8 MiB | Copy unchanged | Copy unchanged |
| Create, append, rename-over, unlink, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, hard link, and symbolic link, seen by the adapter | All seen | All seen | All seen |
| Round trip of a 22-entry tree | 17 captured, content and `mtime` identical | Same | Same |
| Start-up canary written by the supervisor | Seen | Seen | Seen; `oh`: not seen |
| inotify tracker in the adapter, 200 files with renames and the other operations | 207 dirty paths | 0 events | 207 |
| Write through a hard link in another directory | Only the written name reported | Not applicable | Only the written name reported |
| Spool volume with `sizeLimit: 300Mi`, three `FICLONE` copies of 200 MiB | Kubelet counted 600 MiB and evicted the pod, with about no new disk | Not run | Not run |
| Warm clones read-only at one path | `EROFS` on both sides; the adapter's `git fsck` of a slot's `--shared` clone returned 0 | Same | Same |

Under gVisor without hints, `st_dev` differed between the adapter's and the slot's views, 52 and 111. With the pod-share hint both containers shared one sentry mount with device 51, and `statfs` reported each volume. Under runc `statfs` reported the whole node file system. The losses that remained came from the adapter's own file access: files of mode 0600 and the contents of directories of mode 0700, a file of mode 0644 that an atomic save through Python's `tempfile` replaced with mode 0600, hard-link identity, the owner, and widened restored modes. SI decision D9 removes them.

**Spike 3: the file-descriptor broker.** The broker was prototyped in a copy of the supervisor, about 470 added lines of C, 13% of 3,585, with `.text` growing from 17,864 to 21,264 bytes, built freestanding with `-Werror`. It ran under runc, gVisor's default mount, and gVisor's pod-share mount, in a Restricted namespace with no capability. No other candidate the spike tried read every file the slot's owner can open, restored exactly, and kept `FICLONE` and the tracker working on every runtime with one code path. The rejected candidates were a broker container per slot, which saw 1 dirty path on gVisor's default mount because it watches through its own mount and costs about 10 MB of memory per slot under runc, adapter capabilities, which Restricted refuses and gVisor cannot run, a copy-out by the supervisor, which moves the walk, the index, and the racy rule into C, and mode rewriting, which changes modes visibly.

- **Reads.** `getdents` on passed descriptors of directories of mode 0700 and 0500 worked, and so did reads of files of mode 0600 and 0400 and of `mkstemp` files. The adapter's own mount returned `EACCES` for every one.
- **Identity.** `fstat` on a passed descriptor matched `lstat` on device, inode, size, mode, `mtime`, and `ctime` to the nanosecond, and the racy recheck worked. The descriptor's mount ID was the slot's mount, 374, rather than the adapter's, 322, and under gVisor's default mount `st_dev` differed as well, `0:96` against `0:55`.
- **Tracker.** With the inotify instance in the supervisor, the tracker reported 212 dirty paths under runc, with the barrier seen in 0.3 to 0.7 ms, and 211 under gVisor's default mount.
- **Restore.** Owner, mode (0000, 0400, 0500, and 0300 included), nanosecond `mtime`, hard links, sparse extents, FIFOs, and symbolic links came back exactly for 23 of 23 entries, and the slot could `chmod` and touch the results. A restore through the adapter's own mount got 22 of 23 wrong.
- **Extended attributes.** `fgetxattr` and `fsetxattr` on passed descriptors of files of mode 0600 returned `EACCES`.
- **Hostile replies.** The adapter's checks refused 24 of 25 hostile replies on every runtime (S6).
- **What remains.** Files of mode 0000 and directories their owner cannot list are captured as metadata only, sockets are recorded and not restored, device nodes cannot exist, extended attributes are not captured, gVisor reports a sparse file as one data extent, and `FICLONE` returns `ENOTSUP` under gVisor.

Per-file costs for 10,000 files of 4 KiB, in µs, as broker / direct:

| Operation | runc | gVisor default mount | gVisor pod-share mount |
| :-- | --: | --: | --: |
| Walk, open, and read, 253 paths per request | 4.9 / 3.8 | 25.4 / 23.2 | 16.1 / 15.0 |
| Same, 1 path per request | 39.0 | 80.1 | 73.2 |
| Stat-only walk | 2.1 / 0.5 | 15.5 / 5.1 | 10.3 / 2.2 |
| Restore | 9.2 / 5.2 | 43.7 / 23.4 | 24.6 / 11.4 |

Kata was reasoned about and not run. It runs one guest kernel per pod, so the broker, `openat2`, and creation as the owner behave as under runc, and `FICLONE` needs a reflink file system in the guest, which `ext4` on `block-plain` is not.

## Benchmarks

These measurements were taken on AWS between 2026-11-05 and 2026-11-06 with a standalone harness. The harness implements the current method and this design's capture, storage, and restore paths. It runs no Lenny component and uses none of the project's test infrastructure. The scripts, raw results, and analysis are kept outside the repository with the benchmark notes.

Every figure in this section predates the file-descriptor broker of SI decision D9 and the slot layout of SI decision D8. The harness read and wrote the workspace directly, in one container, so the figures stand as measured and are not adjusted. "Figures the broker can change" sets each figure that the broker's per-file cost can move beside its value through the broker from "Re-validation through the broker", and "Validation owed" states what remains to measure.

### Setup

- **Nodes.** EC2 in `us-west-2` on Amazon Linux 2023, with containerd 2.4.2, runc 1.5.2, gVisor `release-20261102.0` on the systrap platform with directfs, and Kata Containers 4.3.1 on QEMU. Most runs used `m8i.2xlarge` with nested virtualization enabled and a gp3 root volume at its baseline of 3,000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s. Other runs used `i4i.2xlarge` with local NVMe and `m7i.metal-24xl` bare metal with gp3.
- **Pods.** Each run started one container through nerdctl, limited to 4 CPUs and 8 GiB or to 2 CPUs and 4 GiB, with the workspace on a host directory, which is how the kubelet backs an `emptyDir`. Kubernetes and the kubelet were not involved.
