- **Workspaces.** The harness cloned each repository from GitHub once, stored it in S3 as a tar, and extracted it into the workspace before each run with the page cache dropped.

  | Workspace | History | Files | Directories | Size |
  | :-- | :-- | --: | --: | --: |
  | Chromium | Depth 1 | 516,434 | 46,969 | 5.9 GiB |
  | LLVM | Full | 189,899 | 17,153 | 6.3 GiB |
  | Linux | Full | 97,281 | Not recorded | 8.1 GiB |

- **Object store.** S3 in the same region, through the AWS SDK. The current method uploads 16 MiB tar chunks one at a time, as `streamChunks` does. This design's path keeps several pack uploads in flight.
- **Quiesce.** The harness stands in for a quiescing runtime by sending `SIGSTOP` to the workload's process group at the start of the capture pass and `SIGCONT` at its end. The pause figures therefore leave out the time a runtime takes to reach `checkpoint_ready`.
- **Scenarios.** "Steady" is a checkpoint after 50 files changed. "After checkout" follows `git checkout HEAD~2000`, on the repositories with full history. "Package install" runs a writer that creates 150,000 files of 4 KiB at 10,000 files per second, and the checkpoint starts 3 seconds after the writer. Other scenarios wrote constantly to a 4 GiB file and ran `git repack -a -d` during the checkpoint.
- **Variants.** "gVisor exclusive" adds `--file-access-mounts=exclusive`, and "gVisor KVM" adds `--platform=kvm`. "Kata metadata" and "Kata always" set `virtio_fs_cache`, whose default is `auto`. "Kata block" places the workspace on an ext4 file system on a block device passed into the guest, which is what Kata's `emptydir_mode = "block-plain"` gives a pod. Kata guests booted with `sysctl.fs.inotify.max_user_watches=1048576` once the guest limit was found, and containerd ran with `LimitNOFILE=1048576` in the final run.
- **Checks.** Every steady, after-checkout, and package-install capture was followed by a verification walk while the workload was still stopped, which counted the changes the capture had missed. Every restore verified each chunk's SHA-256. No run reported here missed a change or restored a wrong byte. An earlier tracker version cleared its overflow flag at each swap, and on Kata, where 31,483 watches had failed, it missed 28 changed files. The persistent degraded state in "Change tracker" is the correction, and the runs after it missed none.

### Current method against this design

The table compares the pause on 4 CPUs on `m8i.2xlarge`. The current method's pause is the time it takes to archive and upload the whole workspace, which is how long a quiescing runtime stays quiesced today. Every value in that column is above the 60-second `CheckpointTimeout`, so in the platform today each of these checkpoints would fail after pausing the runtime for 60 seconds. This design's pause is shown for the stat walk and for the change tracker, for the steady scenario.

| Profile | Workspace | Current method: pause | This design: pause, walk | This design: pause, tracker |
| :-- | :-- | --: | --: | --: |
| runc | Chromium | 225 s | 1.09 s | 0.12 s |
| runc | LLVM | 196 s | 0.30 s | 0.03 s |
| runc | Linux | 322 s | 0.09 s | Not measured |
| gVisor | Chromium | 311 s | 8.59 s | 0.11 s |
| gVisor | LLVM | 284 s | 2.93 s | 0.04 s |
| gVisor | Linux | 459 s | 1.46 s | Not measured |
| Kata | Chromium | 625 s | 6.40 s | 0.16 s |
| Kata | LLVM | 671 s | 2.40 s | 0.06 s |
| Kata | Linux | 1,017 s | 0.60 s | Not measured |

The current method uploaded 2.80 GiB for Chromium, 4.19 GiB for LLVM, and 6.49 GiB for Linux on every checkpoint. This design uploaded 2.97 GiB and 4.31 GiB on the first checkpoint, because zstd at its fastest level compresses less than gzip, and one pack on each steady checkpoint.

The first checkpoint of a session reads and uploads the whole workspace during pre-copy, while the runtime runs. It took 40 to 76 s for Chromium and 18 to 32 s for LLVM under runc, 50 to 113 s under gVisor, and 39 to 83 s under Kata. Its pause was the same as a steady pause, because pre-copy leaves only the files changed during the pass for the capture.

An earlier run on nested `m8i.2xlarge` measured gVisor KVM at 7.55 / 0.04 s for the LLVM steady pause, and Kata always at 2.35 / 0.16 s for Chromium and 0.75 / 0.04 s for LLVM. Kata always matched Kata metadata.

The package-install columns are not a comparison between walk and tracker. The walk run and the tracker run each restarted the writer, and the number of files written by the pause differed between them by up to a factor of 3. What the columns show is the pause for 10,000 to 100,000 freshly written files. In the walk and tracker columns of the same row, the read cost per changed file was the same.

### What the pause consists of

The pause has a change-source part and a read part. These are the per-unit costs measured in the package-install scenario on 4 CPUs.

| Configuration | Stat walk, per file in the workspace | Read, per changed 4 KiB file | Tracker barrier wait during package install |
| :-- | --: | --: | --: |
| runc | 1.6 to 2.1 µs | 5 to 7 µs | 0.2 to 0.3 s |
| gVisor | 15 to 17 µs | 51 to 56 µs | 3.9 to 6.1 s |
| gVisor exclusive | 11 µs | 46 to 49 µs | 3.9 to 4.0 s |
| gVisor KVM, metal | 24 to 27 µs | 47 to 48 µs | 3.5 to 3.7 s |
| Kata | 12 µs | 62 to 69 µs | 0.7 to 1.6 s |
| Kata metadata | 3.9 to 4.6 µs | 116 to 129 µs | 0.5 to 0.9 s |
| Kata metadata, metal | 2.7 to 3.2 µs | 67 to 70 µs | 0.7 to 1.1 s |
| Kata block | 1.6 to 2.6 µs | 6 to 8 µs | 0.2 to 0.4 s |

The tracker removes the stat walk. Under gVisor it replaces the walk with a barrier wait when files are being created at 10,000 per second, because the tracker reads events more slowly than the workload produces them, and the pause waits for the backlog. Under runc and on a Kata block volume both parts stay small.

### Effect of the tracker on the runtime's work

The harness ran each workload six times in alternation, three times without the tracker and three times with it, after a warm-up run. The table gives the medians, in seconds, without / with the tracker.

| Configuration | Create 50,000 files | Delete 50,000 files | `git status`, Chromium | `git checkout HEAD~2000`, LLVM |
| :-- | --: | --: | --: | --: |
| runc | 0.59 / 0.65 (+10%) | 0.32 / 0.37 (+14%) | 0.99 / 0.98 | 2.17 / 2.23 (+3%) |
| gVisor | 3.01 / 5.05 (+68%) | 0.63 / 0.81 (+27%) | 9.41 / 9.49 | 5.68 / 6.21 (+9%) |
| gVisor exclusive | 2.75 / 4.53 (+65%) | 0.61 / 0.75 (+24%) | 7.33 / 7.09 | 4.80 / 5.19 (+8%) |
| gVisor exclusive, 2 CPUs | 2.74 / 6.24 (+128%) | 0.62 / 1.06 (+71%) | 7.52 / 7.41 | 5.22 / 5.56 (+7%) |
| gVisor, metal | 3.14 / 4.52 (+44%) | 0.60 / 0.79 (+32%) | 10.26 / 10.29 | 5.73 / 6.12 (+7%) |
| gVisor KVM, metal | 3.00 / 3.64 (+21%) | 0.91 / 0.99 (+9%) | 13.23 / 13.10 | 7.00 / 7.20 (+3%) |
| Kata | 14.22 / 15.64 (+10%) | 6.77 / 7.25 (+7%) | 19.30 / 19.34 | 11.94 / 12.41 (+4%) |
| Kata metadata | 13.81 / 15.12 (+9%) | 6.75 / 6.85 (+2%) | 6.30 / 6.29 | 8.91 / 9.47 (+6%) |
| Kata block | 0.50 / 0.58 (+16%) | 0.24 / 0.52 (+117%) | 1.15 / 1.25 | 2.22 / 2.85 (+28%) |
| Kata block, 2 CPUs | 0.47 / 0.61 (+30%) | 0.24 / 0.49 (+104%) | 1.20 / 1.20 | 2.22 / 7.35 (+232%) |

Workloads that read files, such as `git status`, are unaffected. Workloads that create or delete many files pay the tracker's event handling, which runs on the pod's CPUs. Under gVisor, creating 50,000 files took 0.6 to 3.5 s longer. On a Kata block volume with 2 CPUs, two of the three checkouts with the tracker took 7.4 s against 2.2 s, which is the largest effect measured.

### Isolation variants

- **gVisor exclusive file access** lowered the stat walk by 30 to 32% and left the read cost unchanged. In Kubernetes it also stopped every kubelet update from reaching the sandbox (see "Kubernetes checks"), so this design rules it out.
- **gVisor's pod-share mount hint** made the stat walk 3.2 times faster than the default mount and restore 3.2 times faster, and it is the only gVisor configuration on which the adapter-held tracker of revision 12 saw writes from another container (see "Kubernetes checks"). The supervisor-held tracker sees them on both mounts, and IC-1, "Change tracker" states why the hint stays.
- **gVisor KVM** made the stat walk slower than systrap on both node types. On bare metal it was 39 to 43% slower, and under nested virtualization it was 2.6 times slower. Tracker setup was 37 to 39% slower on metal. File creation with the tracker running was faster than under systrap, at 3.64 s against 4.52 s for 50,000 files. KVM does not reduce the checkpoint pause, so this design does not recommend it for checkpointing.
- **Kata cache modes.** `virtio_fs_cache = "metadata"` made the stat walk 2.7 to 3.2 times faster than the default and doubled the read cost per small file under nested virtualization. `always` measured the same as `metadata`.
- **Kata block volume** gave the lowest pause of any Kata variant, about the same as runc, and a tracker setup cost close to runc's. The workspace is then a file system inside the guest, so the host cannot read it.
- **Bare metal** lowered Kata's walk pause by up to 31% against nested virtualization. It did not help gVisor, whose pause on `m7i.metal-24xl` was 9 to 15% longer than on `m8i.2xlarge`, which has a newer CPU.

### Sizing

- **CPUs.** With 2 CPUs in place of 4, the steady walk pause grew 11 to 43%. The package-install pause under gVisor grew 1.6 to 2.4 times, and the first checkpoint of Chromium under gVisor took 208 s against 139 s.
- **Disks.** Local NVMe on `i4i.2xlarge` did not lower the pause under runc or gVisor against gp3, because the changed files were in the page cache. It made the first checkpoint faster under runc, at 22 s against 31 s for Chromium.

### Restore

A restore downloads the packs, verifies every chunk, and writes each file once. The tables give the seconds until the restored tree is complete, which is when the runtime can start. The `sync` column is the time a following `sync` took to flush the written data to disk, which a restore does not wait for.

Chromium on `m8i.2xlarge` with 4 CPUs, against today's tar restore in the same container:

| Configuration | Container memory limit | Today's tar restore | This design | `sync` after this design |
| :-- | :-- | --: | --: | --: |
| runc, gp3 at 3,000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s | 8 GiB | 75 s | 159 to 168 s | 8 to 13 s |
| runc, gp3 at 3,000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s | 28 GiB | 59 s | 88 to 96 s | 91 to 97 s |
| runc, gp3 at 16,000 IOPS and 1,000 MiB/s | 8 GiB | 63 s | 32 to 35 s | 3 to 6 s |
| runc, gp3 at 16,000 IOPS and 1,000 MiB/s | 28 GiB | 66 s | 18 to 24 s | 18 to 22 s |
| gVisor, gp3 at 3,000 IOPS | 8 GiB | 102 s | 185 s | Not measured |
| gVisor, gp3 at 16,000 IOPS and 1,000 MiB/s | 8 GiB | 104 s | 45 to 49 s | 1 to 2 s |

Each range covers five restores that applied packs in random order and in path order, with 4, 8, and 16 packs in flight. Neither the order nor the concurrency changed the result. The gp3 baseline of 3,000 IOPS is what limits this design's restore of 516,000 small files, and at 16,000 IOPS the restore took half the time of today's tar restore. A larger memory limit let more of the written data wait in the page cache, which shortened the restore and moved the same work into the flush.

Other configurations, gp3 at its baseline, 4 CPUs and 8 GiB:

| Configuration | Chromium | LLVM |
| :-- | --: | --: |
| runc | 172 s | 56 s |
| gVisor exclusive | 176 s | 62 s |
| Kata | 157 s | 72 s |
| Kata metadata | 137 s | 54 s |
| Kata metadata, metal | 40 s | 21 s |
| Kata block | 75 s | 53 s |
| Kata block, 2 CPUs | 83 s | 40 s |
| Kata block, metal | 73 s | 34 s |

Today's tar restore took 71 to 75 s for Chromium and 75 s for LLVM under runc, 102 s and 87 s under gVisor, and 234 s and 147 s under Kata on the same nodes. LLVM's 6.2 GiB in 190,000 files restored faster than tar on every configuration. Kata's shared file system on bare metal restored fastest. Its writes go through the host-side virtio-fs daemon into the node's page cache, outside the pod's memory limit, which matches the effect of the 28 GiB limit under runc.

### Kubernetes checks

These checks ran on 2026-11-06 on a single-node k3s cluster (kubelet 1.37.2) on `m8i.2xlarge`, using the same containerd, runc, gVisor, and Kata builds, with one RuntimeClass per configuration. The Kata block configuration set `emptydir_mode = "block-plain"` in a configuration file Kata ships, because Kata 4.3.1 refuses a `KATA_CONF_FILE` outside its shipped set.

Inotify across containers. A pod ran the tracker in one container and wrote from a second container into the shared `emptyDir`: 200 new files in a new directory, an append to an existing file, and a rename.

| Configuration | Workspace mount seen in the pod | Dirty paths reported |
| :-- | :-- | --: |
| runc | `xfs` from the node | 202 |
| gVisor | `9p` | 0 |
| gVisor exclusive | `9p` | 0 |
| gVisor, pod-share mount hint | `tmpfs` | 202 |
| gVisor exclusive, pod-share mount hint | `tmpfs` | 202 |
| Kata | `virtiofs` | 0 |
| Kata block-plain | `ext4` on `/dev/sda`, with `discard` | 202 |

The table measured the adapter-held tracker of revision 12, in a container other than the writer's. Under SI decision D9 the tracker's inotify instance belongs to the slot's supervisor, in the writer's own container, and the spike of 2026-11-12 saw its events on gVisor's default mount ("Session isolation spikes"). The table therefore no longer decides where the tracker runs, and IC-8, "Change tracker setting" states the rule.

Kubelet-updated volumes. Each pod mounted a ConfigMap, a Secret, a downward API volume with the pod's labels, and a projected volume with a service account token that expires after 600 s. The test changed the ConfigMap, the Secret, and a label, and read the files every 5 s.

| Configuration | ConfigMap, Secret, and label updates | Token refresh at 80% of its lifetime |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| runc | Seen after 11 s | Refreshed |
| gVisor | Seen after 11 s | Refreshed |
| gVisor exclusive | Not seen in 360 s | Not refreshed by 560 s |
| Kata | Seen after 11 s | Refreshed |

With `--file-access-mounts=exclusive` the sandbox kept serving the files it had cached, so a pod would read its expired token until it restarted.

`emptyDir` size limit. Each pod had an `emptyDir` with `sizeLimit: 1Gi`. It wrote 600 MiB, deleted it and wrote 600 MiB again, and then wrote 600 MiB more.

| Configuration | After 600 MiB | After delete and rewrite | After 1,200 MiB live |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| runc | 600 MiB on the node | 600 MiB | Evicted |
| gVisor | 898 MiB on the node | 600 MiB | Evicted |
| gVisor, pod-share mount hint | 600 MiB in `.gvisor.filestore.<id>` | 600 MiB | Evicted |
| Kata | 600 MiB on the node | 600 MiB | Evicted |
| Kata block-plain | 687 MiB in `disk.img` | 687 MiB | Evicted |

Every configuration was evicted with "Usage of EmptyDir volume "ws" exceeds the limit "1Gi"". The kubelet's usage figure trailed the node's disk usage by up to a minute on Kata and Kata block-plain, so eviction there came that much later. Under gVisor's default mount the node's usage read 898 MiB after 600 MiB had been written, the kubelet counted the 898 MiB, and the excess was gone after the rewrite. On Kata block-plain, a deleted file returned its space through `discard`, and the file system's metadata took 87 MiB of the limit.

gVisor's pod-share mount. The Chromium benchmark ran in Kubernetes under gVisor with 4 CPUs and 8 GiB, once on the default mount and once with the mount hints, with the tracker and the workload in one container.

| Measurement | Default mount (`9p`) | Pod-share mount (`tmpfs`) |
| :-- | --: | --: |
| Steady pause, walk / tracker | 8.70 s / 0.12 s | 2.75 s / 0.13 s |
| Package-install pause, walk / tracker | 14.75 s / 7.32 s | 7.45 s / 4.14 s |
| Parallel stat walk of 516,434 files | 8.43 s | 2.21 s |
| Tracker setup, wall / CPU | 9.5 s / 35.7 s | 2.7 s / 9.3 s |
| Create 50,000 files, without / with the tracker | 3.13 s / 5.41 s | 1.87 s / 2.95 s |
| Delete 50,000 files, without / with the tracker | 0.64 s / 0.80 s | 0.26 s / 0.50 s |
| Restore | 177 s | 56 s |
| Most host descriptors held by the sentry | 113,221 | 777 |

The first checkpoint on the pod-share mount in walk mode took 631 s, of which 577 s was spent waiting for uploads to S3 after the pre-copy read had finished in 40 s. The tracker-mode first checkpoint that followed uploaded the same bytes without waiting, so the stall came from the upload path rather than the mount. Its pause was 2.81 s.

### What the benchmarks do not cover

- A quiescing runtime's own time to quiesce, which adds to every pause.
- A multi-node cluster, and the adapter and runtime images Lenny ships. The Kubernetes checks used generic images on one node.
- Clouds other than AWS, and Arm nodes.
- Upload through presigned URLs and through the gateway's grant protocol.
- The file-descriptor broker, slot containers, the adapter-only spool volume, and pods with several slots.

### Figures the broker can change

The broker adds a cost for each entry the adapter opens, lists, creates, or watches, and none for each byte. Figures that grow with file count can therefore move, and figures that grow with bytes or come from the object store cannot. The third column gives the value measured through the broker on the session isolation layout on 2026-11-12 ("Re-validation through the broker"). The walk pauses give the walk through `open`, through `statat`, and the parallel stat walk of [decision D19](#decisions), which is this design's walk. The re-validation's Chromium has 565,000 entries against 516,000 files in this section.

| Figure | Before, direct | Through the broker, 2026-11-12 |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Steady walk pause, Chromium, runc | 1.09 s | `open` 1.86 s, `statat` 1.67 s, parallel stat walk 1.29 s |
| Steady walk pause, LLVM, runc | 0.30 s | `open` 0.59 s, `statat` 0.50 s, parallel stat walk 0.38 s |
| Steady walk pause, Chromium, gVisor default mount | 8.59 s | `open` 17.24 s, `statat` 10.18 s, both over the 10 s budget in the adapter's estimate, parallel stat walk 6.33 s. On metal `open` 16.82 s and `statat` 9.61 to 9.77 s |
| Steady walk pause, LLVM, gVisor default mount | 2.93 s | `open` 6.08 s, `statat` 3.62 s, parallel stat walk 2.23 s |
| Steady walk pause, Chromium, gVisor pod-share mount | 2.75 s | `open` 8.45 s, `statat` 5.95 s, parallel stat walk 2.55 s |
| Steady walk pause, Chromium, Kata (virtio-fs) | 6.40 s | `open` 57.45 s, `statat` 11.66 s, parallel stat walk 9.44 s, at the edge of the budget |
| Steady walk pause, Chromium and LLVM, Kata block | 1.34 s and 0.30 s | `open` 2.27 s and 0.72 s, `statat` 2.39 s and 0.73 s, parallel stat walk 1.51 s and 0.38 s |
| After-checkout pause, LLVM | runc 0.43 s, gVisor 2.97 s, Kata block 0.42 s, Kata 2.45 s | Parallel stat walk: runc 0.59 s, gVisor default mount 2.25 s, gVisor pod-share 0.84 s, Kata block 0.61 s, Kata 3.40 s |
| Stat walk per entry | runc 1.6 to 2.1 µs, gVisor 15 to 17 µs, Kata 12 µs, Kata block 1.6 to 2.6 µs | Parallel stat walk at 16 threads: runc 0.53 to 0.56 µs, gVisor default mount 9.8 to 10.2 µs, pod-share 3.1 to 3.4 µs, Kata 12.7 to 13.9 µs, Kata block 1.0 to 1.1 µs, which is 1.0 to 1.2 times the adapter's direct walk. `statat` alone: 2.1 to 22.3 µs |
| Pause with the tracker, steady | 0.03 to 0.16 s | 0.013 to 0.094 s for Chromium and LLVM on every configuration, the gVisor default mount at a raised `LimitNOFILE` included |
| Package-install pause, Chromium, walk / tracker | runc 1.40 / 0.42 s, gVisor 13.76 / 5.87 s, pod-share 7.45 / 4.14 s, Kata 7.99 / 2.26 s, Kata block 1.55 / 0.55 s | Walk through `open`: runc 2.15 s, pod-share 12.05 s, Kata 31.66 s, Kata block 3.62 s. Tracker: runc 0.38 s, pod-share 4.97 s, Kata 12.65 s, Kata block 0.69 s |
| Read per changed 4 KiB file | runc 5 to 7 µs, gVisor 51 to 56 µs, Kata 62 to 69 µs, Kata block 6 to 8 µs | runc 8 µs, gVisor default mount 48 µs, pod-share 37 µs, Kata 264 µs, Kata block 10 to 14 µs, wall time per file with 8 readers |
| Tracker barrier wait during package install | runc 0.2 to 0.3 s, gVisor 3.5 to 6.1 s, Kata 0.5 to 1.6 s | 1 to 6 ms on every runtime |
| Tracker setup, Chromium | 0.2 to 1.8 s (runc, Kata block), 2.4 to 14.5 s (gVisor), 2.7 to 8.4 s (Kata) | runc 1.44 s, Kata block 2.0 s, gVisor pod-share 6.4 s, gVisor default mount 11.7 s, Kata 12.9 s |
| Tracker memory | Up to 480 MiB during setup, 35 to 130 MiB settled | Not separable: the adapter's memory was dominated by the file index, 1.0 to 1.3 GiB with Chromium's |
| Tracker's effect on creating and deleting 50,000 files | runc +10% and +14%, gVisor +68% and +27%, Kata block +16% and +117% | runc +40% and +10%, gVisor default mount +70% and +28%, pod-share +32% and +31%, Kata block +65% and +100% |
| First checkpoint, Chromium | runc 40 to 76 s, gVisor 50 to 113 s, Kata 39 to 83 s | runc 27.7 s, gVisor default mount 57.8 s, pod-share 45.2 s, Kata block 40.1 s, Kata 247 s, the last bound by broker `open` through virtio-fs |
| Restore, Chromium, gp3 baseline | runc 159 to 172 s, gVisor 185 s, Kata block 75 s | runc 210 s (205 s writing directly), gVisor default mount 235 s (235 s), pod-share 64 s (65 s), Kata block 77.5 s with one virtual machine on the node |
| Restore, Chromium, 16,000 IOPS | runc 32 to 35 s, gVisor 45 to 49 s | runc 73 s (72 s), gVisor default mount 148 s (146 s), pod-share 33 s (27 s) |
| Restore, LLVM | runc 56 s, Kata block 53 s, gp3 baseline | runc 122 s and 32.5 s at the baseline and at 16,000 IOPS, gVisor default mount 125 to 140 s and 59 s, pod-share 69 s and 19.6 s, Kata block 76 to 86 s and 31 s, each within 1.0 to 1.2 times writing directly |
| `FICLONE` of the 4 GiB file under constant writes, runc | 2.0 to 2.5 s | 11.0 s inside an 11.5 to 11.7 s pause, then 0.5 s at the next checkpoint. With the spool's `sizeLimit` equal to the cap, the kubelet evicted the pod |
| Bytes uploaded on the first checkpoint, Chromium and LLVM | 2.97 GiB and 4.31 GiB | 2.99 GiB and 4.50 GiB, for workspaces cloned from later upstream commits |

The restore figures written directly in the third column come from the re-validation harness on the same node, whose writer was slower than the harness of "Benchmarks" under runc, 72 s against 32 to 35 s at 16,000 IOPS. The gap lies in that writer, because the broker and direct restores on the same node took the same time. The statements in "Restore" that this design restored faster than today's tar restore compare a direct restore with a direct tar restore, and neither path reads the workspace directly under SI decision D9.

### Validation owed

The AWS runs of 2026-11-12 settled decisions D16 to D19 and most items the re-run owed. These remain:

1. Pods of 2 and 4 slots that checkpoint together, with and without the checkpoint stagger of [decision D20](#decisions), under runc and gVisor on workspaces of LLVM and Chromium size: each slot's pause, the time until every slot holds a fresh snapshot, and the cost of concurrent restores on one pod, which the queue does not cover (D20).
2. The parallel stat walk with the tracker, on a pod with 2 slots at workspace scale, and with directory renames, deletions, and symbolic link swaps during the walk, which the local spike tested and the AWS runs did not.
3. The cap on fallback requests, `walkFallbackConcurrency`, on Kata's shared file system with a tree the adapter cannot search.
4. The cause of the 11.0 s `FICLONE` of the 4 GiB file under constant writes under runc, against 2.0 to 2.5 s in "Benchmarks" and 0.5 s at the next checkpoint, and how the pause estimate's clone term should measure it.
5. The spool headroom: fills up to the adapter's usable share on gVisor's default mount with the spool's `sizeLimit` at twice that share, with no eviction.
6. A frozen export of a file within the export share that the runtime rewrites during the export, in copy mode under gVisor. The AWS run rewrote a 1 GiB file, which is over the share.
7. FIFOs on gVisor's default mount captured as metadata-only entries and restored with `mkfifo`.
8. The tracker's watch budget under runc with user namespaces, on 4 and more pods per node with Chromium-sized workspaces.
9. The capture copy, `restore-complete`, and a running `git repack -a -d` on LLVM during a checkpoint, through the asynchronous worker. The AWS run could not measure them, because the synchronous worker held the broker past its deadline.
10. Restore with memory limits of 28 GiB and with 4 and 16 packs in flight, Kata's metadata cache mode, and the first checkpoint on local NVMe.
11. The disk-backed `/session` tree: its capture, its clone into the spool under runc, and its restore beside `/workspace`.
12. Platform chunk sets ([decision D1](#decisions)): two pods that stage the same commit of a store produce identical chunk IDs under runc, gVisor, and Kata, with the normal checkout and with the reflink rebuild of WM-15.
13. The first checkpoint of a Chromium session that adopted a warm-up checkout, with and without the store's platform chunk set: the bytes uploaded, the pre-copy time, and the files the first pre-copy reads after the index is seeded.
14. The adapter's memory for the chunk lists of the contributions for its pod's staged content, for Chromium and LLVM, on pods of 1 and 4 slots.
15. The extra time before READY on a pod that contributes a Chromium-sized tree for a new store and for a new commit of an existing store.
16. The owner's required test on pods with several runtimes, planned once the designs settle: file copying in both directions (materialization, upload staging, capture, restore, and exports), the warm git stores and slot clones (warm-up checkout and the reflink rebuild), the checkpoint stagger, and volume and memory sizing at the defaults, `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` of 10 GiB and the shared 16 GiB spool among them.
17. Decision D11's margin: on each runtime, how often a capture estimated within the budget with the margin overran the budget, and how many captures the margin sent to the best-effort path that would have fitted.

## Re-validation through the broker

Two runs on AWS on 2026-11-12 measured this design through the file-descriptor broker on the session isolation layout, as the owner asked after SI decisions D8 and D9. The first re-ran "Benchmarks" through the broker and settled decisions D16 to D18. The second measured Kata `block-plain` with a dedicated `fsGroup` and the parallel stat walk that decision D19 adopts. They cost about $8.27 and $1.77, and every resource of both was deleted, with the deletion verified by tag and by ID. Their logs lived in a session scratchpad that does not persist, so this section holds every result this design relies on.

### Setup

- **Harness.** A new standalone harness in Go implements IC-1's passes, IC-2's chunks, packs, and manifest, and IC-5's restore, and reaches the slot's files only through the broker. It runs no Lenny component and uses none of the project's test infrastructure. Its runtime stand-in, launched by the supervisor in each slot container, stops the workload's process groups with `SIGSTOP` on `checkpoint_request` and answers `checkpoint_ready`, as in "Benchmarks". The supervisor is the tree's C supervisor with spike 3's broker, named broker roots, and a prototype of `statat` that answers 11 integers per path and passes no descriptor. The broker client pipelines requests, applies S6's checks, and gives each request a 120 s deadline. When the estimate was over budget, the harness quiesced anyway so that the pause was measured, and recorded the decision the adapter would have taken.
- **Checks.** After every capture, while the workload was still stopped, a verification walk in the slot container's own mount view compared every entry's inode, mode, size, `mtime`, `ctime`, and link target with the capture's index. A fidelity check compared the source and restored trees on type, mode, owner, size, nanosecond `mtime`, content, link target, sparse extents, and link group, over a coverage set of modes 0000, 0400, 0500, 0300, and 0700, a `mkstemp` file, a FIFO, symbolic links, two link groups, a 64 MiB sparse file with 3 data extents, and a file name of invalid UTF-8.
- **Nodes.** `m8i.2xlarge` with nested virtualization and `m7i.metal-24xl` in `us-west-2`, on Amazon Linux 2023 with kernel 6.12, containerd 2.4.2 with `LimitNOFILE=65535` unless a row says otherwise, runc 1.5.3, gVisor `release-20261109.0` on systrap with directfs and `dev.gvisor.*` annotations passed through, and Kata Containers 4.3.1 with runtime-rs, QEMU 11.0.1, and guest kernel 6.18.56, on virtio-fs with `virtio_fs_cache = "auto"` and on `block-plain`, both with the guest watch limit raised. Each node ran single-node k3s v1.37.2, with the data on a gp3 volume formatted XFS with `reflink=1`.
- **Pods.** The adapter under UID 10000 and one slot container per slot under UID 20000 + n, with whole-volume slot mounts, an adapter-only `lenny-spool` with `sizeLimit` of 512Mi per slot, Restricted Pod Security admission, every capability dropped, and no `fsGroup` except on Kata `block-plain`. gVisor's pod-share configuration put the hint on the slot workspace volumes and the spool. The slot container had 4 CPUs and 8 GiB, so the CPU available to the walk differs from "Benchmarks".
- **Workspaces.** Chromium at depth 1, 564,882 entries in 47,105 directories, LLVM with full history, 208,044 entries, and Linux with full history, 103,745 entries, each cloned from GitHub on 2026-11-12, plus flat directories of 100,000 and 1,000,000 entries in the second run.

### Correctness

- 421 of 424 consistent captures missed no change, on every runtime, mount, scenario, and workspace. The other 3 were captures on gVisor's default mount of a tree holding a FIFO, which S6's device check refused, so the snapshot left the FIFO out without an error. IC-1 now records such a FIFO as a metadata-only entry.
- Best-effort captures of the 4 GiB file under constant writes flagged it `torn`, as R8 requires.
- No restore wrote a wrong byte: every chunk's SHA-256 verified.
- Broker restores were exact under runc and on gVisor's pod-share mount, over 564,902 and 208,064 entries with the coverage set. On Kata `block-plain` with `fsGroup` the only differences were 4 set-group-ID bits on directories, which the broker's mode mask drops.
- The restore written through the adapter's own mount, kept as a baseline, gave every entry the adapter's UID, made the 0000 and 0400 files and the `mkstemp` file unreadable to the slot, and lost the entries under the 0300 directory, the losses SI decision D9 removes.
- In the second run every checkpoint missed no change, and the identities that the parallel stat walk read matched broker `statat`, broker `open`, and the slot's own `lstat` walk on device, inode, mode, link count, size, `mtime`, and `ctime` for every entry, 208,084 to 1,000,002 per tree, on every runtime.

### The walk

Steady pause in seconds with 50 changed files, as the median of the repetitions. "Open" and "statat" are the first run's walks, with 8 threads, and "parallel" is the second run's parallel stat walk with 16 threads.

| Configuration | Chromium: open / statat / parallel | LLVM: open / statat / parallel | Linux: open / statat |
| :-- | --: | --: | --: |
| runc | 1.86 / 1.67 / 1.29 | 0.59 / 0.50 / 0.38 | 0.27 / 0.23 |
| gVisor default mount | 17.24 / 10.18 / 6.33 | 6.08 / 3.62 / 2.23 | 2.93 / 1.63 |
| gVisor pod-share mount | 8.45 / 5.95 / 2.55 | 3.03 / 2.10 / 0.81 | 1.45 / 0.94 |
| Kata block-plain | 2.27 / 2.39 / 1.51 | 0.72 / 0.73 / 0.38 | 0.30 / 0.29 |
| Kata virtio-fs | 57.45 / 11.66 / 9.44 | 21.80 / 5.07 / 3.50 | 10.35 / 1.69 |
| gVisor default mount, metal | 16.82 / 9.62 / not run | 5.91 / 3.43 / not run | Not run |
| Kata block-plain, metal | 1.91 / 2.09 / not run | 0.57 / 0.57 / not run | Not run |

- **The supervisor's one thread bounds a walk through the broker.** With 4 or 16 pipelined callers, the broker served `statat` at about 1.3 µs per path and `open` at 2.0 µs under runc, against 3.1 and 3.6 µs with 1 caller, and adding walker threads past 4 gained nothing. Under gVisor's default mount the flat rates were 11 and 24 µs, and on Kata's shared file system 57 and 147 µs, where every broker `open` is an open through virtiofsd.
- **`statat`** cut the walk by 41% on gVisor's default mount, 30% on the pod-share mount, 80% on Kata's shared file system, and 10% under runc, and did not help on Kata `block-plain`. With it Chromium still paused 10.13 to 10.24 s on gVisor's default mount and 11.5 to 11.8 s on Kata's shared file system, and the adapter's estimate sent those captures to the best-effort path. The walk through `open` added 71% to Chromium's pause under runc and 97% to LLVM's, against decision D16's bound of 50%, and put Chromium on gVisor's default mount at 17.2 s, so both of D16's criteria were met.
- **The parallel stat walk** sent one broker request per walk with no `EACCES`, and its worst broker round trip fell from 0.5 to 76 ms with `statat` to 0.1 to 0.5 ms. At 4 threads it got most of its gain under runc, on gVisor's pod-share mount, and on both Kata file systems. Under gVisor's default mount the thread count barely mattered, because the gofer serializes, and the direct walk behaved the same way.
- **R1.** Chromium under runc paused 1.29 s with the parallel stat walk, against R1's 1 s for 500,000 files. The walk itself cost about what the direct walk's 0.31 s costs, and the comparison of 565,000 entries against the index cost about 1 s in the harness. With the tracker, Chromium paused 0.032 to 0.041 s under runc.
- **Flat directories.** A flat directory of 1,000,000 entries paused 1.6 to 2.0 s under runc and on Kata `block-plain`, 3.4 to 4.3 s on gVisor's pod-share mount, 9.1 to 10.6 s on gVisor's default mount, and 22 to 23 s on Kata's shared file system with 4 or more threads, and the last two fell to best effort in some or all repetitions. In every runtime most of the pause was the comparison against the index.
- **Fallback.** A directory of mode 0700 or 0750 denied the adapter's search under runc, gVisor, and Kata's shared file system, where the guest enforces the permission. A flat directory of 1,000,000 entries of mode 0700 sent every entry to `statat` and cost per entry about what the `statat` walk costs. Its worst broker requests were 4.6 to 53 ms under runc and gVisor, 11 to 14 ms on Kata `block-plain`, and 146 to 370 ms on Kata's shared file system with 8 and 16 threads, against 14 to 20 ms for a `statat` walk.
- **Identity.** On Kata's shared file system a `statx` relative to a broker-passed directory descriptor returned the slot's device `0:57` and the pinned mount ID 186, where the adapter's own mount reports `0:49` and 156. Under gVisor's default mount the device differs as well, `0:69` against `0:107`. Under runc, on Kata `block-plain`, and on gVisor's pod-share mount only the mount ID differs.

### The tracker

| Configuration | Chromium setup | Chromium steady pause | LLVM steady pause | Chromium package-install pause |
| :-- | --: | --: | --: | --: |
| runc | 1.44 s | 0.035 s | 0.013 s | 0.38 s |
| gVisor default mount | 11.7 s | 0.041 s at `LimitNOFILE` 1,048,576 | 0.017 s | Not run at Chromium scale |
| gVisor pod-share mount | 6.4 s | 0.041 s | 0.029 s | 4.97 s |
| Kata block-plain | 2.0 s | 0.043 s | 0.013 s | 0.69 s |
| Kata virtio-fs | 12.9 s | 0.094 s | 0.032 s | 12.65 s |

- The barrier wait was 0.5 to 26 ms in steady state and 1 to 6 ms during sustained creation on every runtime. The adapter's event reader cost 0.4 to 1.4 µs per event and kept up with 10,000 creations per second through 0.5 to 0.9 million events per run.
- The verification walk and the pre-copy cross-check found no missed change on any file system, Kata's shared file system and gVisor's default mount included.
- On gVisor's default mount at `LimitNOFILE` 65,535, tracker setup with 47,106 watches succeeded, the sentry then held 65,530 to 65,535 host descriptors, and the next walk failed with `EMFILE` on 27,192 entries. The adapter could then not open its own spool file, `kubectl exec` failed in both containers, and the pod could be removed only by force. Nothing reported an error. At 1,048,576 the sentry peaked at 96,422 descriptors and the tracker worked. LLVM and Linux ran at 65,535 without error. The pod-share mount's sentry held at most 211 descriptors.
- **Queue.** After a 20 s reader stall and 300,000 creates in one watched directory, runc and Kata `block-plain` delivered exactly 16,385 events, the queue's limit of 16,384 and `IN_Q_OVERFLOW`, and Kata's shared file system overflowed during the stall. gVisor exposes no inotify limits, and on both mounts it delivered all 600,000 events after the stall with no overflow. After an overflow the next checkpoint walked and missed no change, and so did the checkpoint after it.
- **Watch budget without user namespaces.** Four runc pods with slot UID 20000 and Chromium's 47,106 watches each held 188,424 watches against the node's `max_user_watches` of 251,978, with none failing.
- **Effect on the workload**, without / with the tracker, as the median of 3: creating 50,000 files took 0.55 / 0.77 s under runc, 2.50 / 4.25 s on gVisor's default mount, 1.57 / 2.07 s on the pod-share mount, and 0.93 / 1.53 s on Kata `block-plain`. Deleting them took 0.40 / 0.44 to 0.49 s, 0.76 / 0.97 s, 0.26 / 0.34 s, and 0.23 / 0.47 s. `git checkout HEAD~2000` on LLVM took 2.33 / 2.38 s under runc and 2.49 / 2.95 s on Kata `block-plain`. `git status` was unaffected.

### Restore

| Configuration and disk | Chromium, broker / direct | LLVM, broker / direct |
| :-- | --: | --: |
| runc, gp3 baseline | 210 / 205 s | 122 / 121 s |
| runc, 16,000 IOPS | 72.9 / 72.4 s | 32.5 / 32.2 s |
| gVisor default mount, gp3 baseline | 235 / 235 s | 125 to 140 / 134 s |
| gVisor default mount, 16,000 IOPS | 148 / 146 s | 59 / 60 s |
| gVisor pod-share mount, gp3 baseline | 64.1 / 64.6 s | 68.6 to 69.4 / 69.2 s |
| gVisor pod-share mount, 16,000 IOPS | 33.0 / 27.1 s | 19.6 / 16.8 s |
| Kata block-plain, gp3 baseline | 77.5 s / not run | 76.4 / 79.4 s |
| Kata block-plain, 16,000 IOPS | Not run | 31.0 / 32.2 s |

- "Direct" is the same restore code writing through the adapter's own mount on the same node. The broker cost 1.0 to 1.2 times the direct path, because the disk or gVisor's gofer bounds the restore.
- The identity pass after a restore took 1.5 to 1.6 s for Chromium under runc, 10.4 to 10.5 s on gVisor's default mount, and 5.9 s on the pod-share mount, and the `utimens` and `chmod` steps took 2.5, 17, and 8 s. The first pre-copy after every broker restore read 0 files.
- In the first run, Kata `block-plain` restores ended with the virtual machine exiting while the source pod's virtual machine still ran on the 32 GiB node. In the second run, with one virtual machine on the node at a time, Chromium and LLVM restored with the host keeping 19 GiB or more available and QEMU's resident memory peaking at 10.5 GiB.

### Capture, spool, and exports

- **Spool modes.** The probe found reflink mode under runc on XFS, copy mode on both gVisor mounts, and read mode on Kata `block-plain`, where the spool and the slot volume are separate file systems in the guest. `copy_file_range` from a passed descriptor copied 256 MiB in 0.056 s on gVisor's pod-share mount and 128 MiB in 0.026 to 0.037 s on both mounts. Counting by the larger of logical and allocated size gave the same numbers as logical size for copies.
- **4 GiB file under constant writes.** Under runc the clone from the passed descriptor took 11.0 s inside an 11.5 to 11.7 s pause, consistent with 0 missed changes, and 0.5 s at the next checkpoint. A 256 MiB export clone of a freshly written file took 1.05 s. Under gVisor and on Kata `block-plain` the file was over the cap, the estimate stayed over budget after 3 rounds, and the capture fell to best effort.
- **Evictions.** The kubelet evicted the pod for exceeding `lenny-spool`'s `sizeLimit` of 512Mi when a reflink clone of the 4 GiB file was counted at full size under runc, when copies filled the spool to exactly the cap on gVisor's default mount, and at a fill of 75% of the cap on gVisor's default mount. A fill of 75% held for 90 s under runc, on the pod-share mount, and on Kata `block-plain`.
- **Exports.** A 256 MiB file was frozen by `copy_file_range` in 0.056 s on gVisor's pod-share mount and then read and packed in 1.9 s. Files of 1 GiB and 5 GiB, over the export share, were read in place in 7.6 s and 36 s. A 1 GiB file the runtime rewrote during its export failed MD-18's identity check with `file_changed` on gVisor's pod-share mount and on Kata `block-plain`.
- **First checkpoint.** Pre-copy read and uploaded the whole workspace in 27.7 s for Chromium and 33.1 s for LLVM under runc, 57.8 s and 43.7 s on gVisor's default mount, 45.2 s and 40.9 s on the pod-share mount, 40.1 s and 40.7 s on Kata `block-plain`, and 247 s and 79.8 s on Kata's shared file system, where broker `open` through virtiofsd bounds it.

### Slots in one pod

- **Two slots checkpointing together**, LLVM: under runc each slot paused 0.68 to 0.72 s against 0.54 to 0.65 s alone. On gVisor's default mount each paused 10.9 to 11.5 s through `open` against 6.1 to 6.5 s alone, and 5.8 to 6.1 s through `statat` against 3.6 to 3.7 s. On the pod-share mount they paused 4.9 to 5.3 s against 3.0 s through `open`, and 3.0 s against 2.1 s through `statat`. Slots in one sandbox share the sentry, and their walks slowed each other by 1.4 to 1.8 times. No change was missed, and every restored entry belonged to the restoring slot's UID.
- **Two restores together**, LLVM at the gp3 baseline, took 283 s each under runc against 120 s alone, 273 s on gVisor's default mount against 132 s, and 241 to 244 s on the pod-share mount against 47 s.
- **Four 1-slot pods against one 4-slot pod**, with idle sessions: one 4-slot pod used about 20 MiB less memory outside the slot containers under runc, about 70 MiB less under gVisor, and 0.65 to 0.85 GiB less on Kata, cut the warm repository store's disk and fill time 4 times, 4.46 GB in 57 to 67 s against 17.8 GB in 146 to 165 s, and let one busy session use 2.14 times the CPU under runc and Kata through pod-level resources. Cold start and bind latency did not change. [`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md) records what these figures decide.

### Git steps through the supervisor's worker

On LLVM under runc, with the worker running synchronously as it did then, a no-op command took 12 ms, and a detached checkout over warm-store alternates took 19.2 s while a broker request sent 0.1 s after it waited 19.1 s. The capture copy, a `git repack -a -d -l`, did not finish within the adapter's 120 s broker deadline, and the adapter declared the slot dead. The same happened on Linux. The owner then approved an asynchronous worker that keeps serving the broker during a run, which [`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md) and [`0153-workspace-materialization`](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md) state.

### Kata block-plain with a dedicated fsGroup

- Without `fsGroup`, Kata `block-plain` volume roots came up `root:root 0755`, which the slot UIDs cannot write. With a dedicated GID, 29000, Kata applies the GID and the set-group-ID bit to every `emptyDir`: disk roots come up `root:29000 2775`, `lost+found` comes up `2770`, and every process in every container carries the GID.
- Slots stayed apart: no path in one slot reached another slot's volumes, and the GID opened another slot's object only when its owner assigned the object to the GID in `/dev/shm` or System V IPC, which mode 0666 already allows. Every slot could read files of mode 0640 and 0660 in the shared read-only volumes, and the adapter could read and write group-accessible slot files through its own mount, which this design does not use.
- The broker reached every file of both slots, with 0 missed changes and 0 identity differences. The release left every volume empty once the Sweep was patched, and `lost+found` was emptied by the Sweep rather than skipped.
- The Sweep in the tree has three defects, which also apply under runc and which [`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md) owns: it skips the slot workspace volume, it fails on slot-owned directories of mode 0500, 0300, or 0000, and its bound of 200,000 entries per volume fails the release of an LLVM-sized session of 208,088 entries.

### What the runs did not cover

- The quiesce time of a real runtime. The stand-in stopped the workload with `SIGSTOP`, as in "Benchmarks".
- Upload and restore through presigned URLs and the gateway's grant protocol.
- Pods of 4 slots checkpointing together, the stagger of decision D20, and the parallel stat walk with the tracker or on a 2-slot pod at workspace scale.
- The capture copy, restore completion, and a repack during a checkpoint through an asynchronous worker.
- User namespaces on runc pools.
- Restore with 28 GiB memory limits and 4 or 16 packs in flight, Kata's metadata cache mode, and the first checkpoint on local NVMe.
- The disk-backed `/session` tree, which the harness did not have.

## Levers

The pause, its frequency, and the cost of checkpoints depend on settings held by the deployer, the operator, the runtime author, and the tenant. The deployer installs the chart and owns the nodes. The operator defines pools and grants them to tenants. The runtime author writes the Runtime definition and the runtime. The tenant chooses among the runtimes and pools it has access to and, under `0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`, derives its own runtimes. The measured effects come from "Re-validation through the broker" where it measured them, and otherwise from "Benchmarks".

### Whether to checkpoint and how often

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `checkpointPolicy: sealOnly` or `none` (IC-8) | Operator on the pool, runtime author on the Runtime, tenant on a derived runtime | No checkpoint runs during the session, so the runtime never pauses for one. | The pause and its upload are removed. | A pod failure or eviction resumes the session without its workspace. The session never becomes dormant, so it holds its pod while idle and expires at the idle timeout. Under `none` the session also has no final snapshot. |
| `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds` (IC-8) | Operator on the pool, runtime author on the Runtime, and tenant on a derived runtime at or above the base's interval, all within the deployer's `checkpoint.minIntervalSeconds` and `checkpoint.maxIntervalSeconds` | Sets how often the pause occurs. | The share of time paused is the pause divided by the interval. For Chromium under gVisor that is 8.6 s in 600 s, or 1.4%, with the stat walk, and 0.11 s in 600 s, or 0.02%, with the tracker. At a 60-second interval the walk pause is 14% of the time. | A longer interval loses more work on a pod failure. |
| The `checkpoint` capability | Runtime author | A runtime that does not declare `checkpoint` in its handshake is captured on the best-effort path while it runs, with no pause. A runtime that declares it pauses for a consistent snapshot. | A best-effort capture has no pause. | The snapshot is tagged `consistency: best-effort`, and a file written during the capture is flagged `torn`. |

### Where the session runs

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| A runc pool on dedicated nodes | Operator: a pool with `isolationProfile: standard` and `allowStandardIsolation: true`, on standard-class nodes with dedicated tenancy for one tenant ([PM-1](../0136-placement/change.md#pm-1-node-classes-and-presets), [PM-2](../0136-placement/change.md#pm-2-node-tenancy)) | Moves the tenant's sessions to the profile with the lowest pause, and makes `FICLONE` available for large changed files on XFS nodes. | Through the broker, Chromium's walk pause is 1.29 s against 6.33 s on gVisor's default mount, 2.55 s on its pod-share mount, and 9.44 s on Kata's shared file system, and the tracker's pause is 0.035 s. | runc shares the host kernel and gives no protection against kernel exploits. Its blast radius is bounded by node separation, which keeps gVisor, Kata, and Lenny system pods off standard-class nodes, by user namespaces, which runc pools require, and by dedicated tenancy, which keeps other tenants' pods off the nodes. The choice of profile is the operator's, through the explicit opt-in `allowStandardIsolation` ([§5.3 Isolation Profiles](../../spec/05_runtime-registry-and-pool-model.md#53-isolation-profiles), as `0136-placement` restates it). |
| CPU allocation | Operator, through the pool's resource class | The capture runs on the pod's CPUs. | 2 CPUs in place of 4 lengthened the steady walk pause by 11 to 43% and the gVisor package-install pause by 1.6 to 2.4 times. | Node capacity. |
| Node disk | Deployer | Affects the first checkpoint and restore, which read or write the whole workspace. | A gp3 volume at 16,000 IOPS and 1,000 MiB/s restored Chromium in 32 to 35 s under runc and 45 to 49 s under gVisor, against 159 to 168 s and 185 s at the gp3 baseline of 3,000 IOPS and 125 MiB/s. Local NVMe made Chromium's first checkpoint under runc 22 s against 31 s on gp3. Neither changed the pause. | gp3 charges for IOPS above 3,000 and throughput above 125 MiB/s, and NVMe instance types cost more. |

### Node configuration for gVisor

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Pass `dev.gvisor.*` pod annotations to runsc | Deployer, with `pod_annotations = ['dev.gvisor.*']` on the containerd runtime handler for gVisor | Lets the mount hints that the pod controller renders on every pod of a pool with `isolationProfile: sandboxed` reach runsc, so each slot's workspace and session volumes and the spool volume become sentry-owned mounts shared by the pod's containers. Without it the slot's mount reports `9p`, the tracker stays off under decision D17, and the capture walks at the default mount's per-entry costs. | Through the broker, Chromium's steady pause with the walk was 2.55 s on the pod-share mount against 6.33 s on the default mount, and 0.041 s with the tracker, which `auto` runs only on the pod-share mount. Chromium restored in 64 s against 235 s at the gp3 baseline, and the sentry held at most 211 host descriptors. "Benchmarks" gives the direct figures: 8.70 s against 2.75 s with the walk, and 177 s against 56 s for a restore. | The workspace is stored on the node as one `.gvisor.filestore.<id>` file, so the node cannot read its files. Managed gVisor offerings that do not pass the annotations keep the walk. |
| `--file-access-mounts=exclusive` | Do not set | Lets the sentry cache file metadata for every mount of the sandbox. | The stat walk was 30 to 32% faster. In Kubernetes, ConfigMap, Secret, and downward API updates never reached the sandbox, and the projected service account token was not refreshed, so a pod keeps an expired token. | Ruled out. The pod-share mount hint gives a larger gain on the workspace volume alone. |
| `--platform=kvm` | Deployer | Replaces systrap with KVM for system call interception. | The stat walk was 39 to 43% slower on bare metal and 2.6 times slower under nested virtualization, and tracker setup was 37 to 39% slower. | Not recommended for checkpointing. It requires bare metal or nested virtualization. |

### Node configuration for runc

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `fs.inotify.max_user_watches` and `fs.inotify.max_user_instances` | Deployer, with a sysctl on runc nodes | Raises the watch and instance budget of each host UID. Under the user namespaces that runc pools require, each pod's slot UIDs map to host UIDs of the pod's own, so each tracker draws on its own budget. | Without user namespaces, four pods with Chromium's 47,106 watches in one slot index held 188,424 watches against the node's `max_user_watches` of 251,978, so a sixth would have exceeded it. Past the limit, trackers fall back to the walk, at 1.29 s for Chromium in place of 0.035 s. | Kernel memory for the watches. The kernel's default gives the watch limit 1% of memory, about 1.3 KiB per watch. |

### Node configuration for Kata

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `kernel_params = "... sysctl.fs.inotify.max_user_watches=1048576"` | Deployer, in the Kata `configuration.toml` | Raises the guest's watch limit, which a 2 GiB guest sets to 15,514. The tracker runs on both of Kata's file systems under `auto`, so it matters on every Kata node. | With the higher limit, Chromium's 47,106 watches succeeded on both file systems. | Guest kernel memory for the watches. |
| `emptydir_mode = "block-plain"` | Deployer, in the Kata `configuration.toml` | Places each `emptyDir` on a block device with an `ext4` file system inside the guest, mounted with `discard`, in place of the shared file system. The pool sets a dedicated `fsGroup`, because the volume roots are otherwise `root:root 0755` ([`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md), the Kata `block-plain` decision). | The lowest Kata pause measured. Through the broker Chromium paused 1.51 s with the walk and 0.043 s with the tracker, against 9.44 s and 0.094 s on the shared file system, and its package install paused 0.69 s against 12.65 s with the tracker. Tracker setup took 2.0 s against 12.9 s, and Chromium restored in 77.5 s. In Kubernetes the kubelet evicted a pod that exceeded its `emptyDir` `sizeLimit`, and deleted files returned their space to the node. | The host cannot read the workspace. The backing file, `disk.img`, has an apparent size of 100 GiB, so `df` inside the guest reports space the `sizeLimit` does not grant. The `ext4` metadata took 87 MiB of the limit. The spool is in read mode, so a changed file is read rather than cloned or copied. A broker restore drops the set-group-ID bit from directories. `block-encrypted` is the variant with encryption at rest. |
| `virtio_fs_cache = "metadata"` | Deployer, in the Kata `configuration.toml` | Lets the guest cache file metadata from the shared file system. | The stat walk was 2.7 to 3.2 times faster than the default, and reading small changed files was about 2 times slower under nested virtualization. `always` measured the same. | The guest sees changes made from the host side of a shared volume later. |
| Bare-metal nodes | Deployer | Removes nested virtualization. | The Kata walk pause was up to 31% lower. | Instance type and price. |

### The workspace and the pause budget

| Lever | Set by | Effect | Measured effect | Cost |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `checkpointExcludes` ([D5](#decisions)) | Runtime author on the Runtime, where a change creates a revision that shares its pod set, and tenant on a derived runtime, appended to the selected base revision's list (IC-8, "Runtime fields and revisions") | Leaves regenerable directories such as `node_modules/` and build outputs out of every snapshot. | The stat walk and tracker setup scale with the number of files and directories, at the per-file costs in "Benchmarks". | The excluded files are absent after a restore and must be regenerated. |
| Git maintenance off ([D6](#decisions)) | Platform default in the rendered git configuration, git's system scope ([`0127-egress-and-git`, "Git configuration"](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#git-configuration), its decision 59). A runtime author overrides it in the runtime's global or repository git configuration, or with `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`. | Keeps automatic `git gc` and `git maintenance` from rewriting packfiles. | After a repack, the next checkpoint uploaded the repository's history again: 16 to 54 s for LLVM and Linux against 1 to 10 s for a steady checkpoint. | Repositories grow loose objects until something repacks them. A runtime that turns maintenance back on, or runs `git gc`, pays the history upload at its own session's next checkpoint. |
| `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` (default 10 GiB, [D3](#decisions)) and `spoolSizeLimitBytes` (default 16 GiB, WM O9) | Operator on the pool | Bound a slot's workspace and the pod's spool. Each adds its `sizeLimit` to the pod's `ephemeral-storage` request. | None on the pause. | A larger limit reserves more node disk per pod and needs a larger namespace ResourceQuota. A smaller workspace limit fails sessions that clone large repositories, and a smaller spool leaves less staging space for uploads and imports. |
| `checkpointPauseBudgetSeconds` (default 10) and `checkpointPreCopyRounds` (default 3) on the pool | Operator on the pool, below 60 s | Bounds the pause. A capture whose estimate times 1.5 exceeds the budget takes another pre-copy round, and after the last round, or at once when the walk alone exceeds the budget, it runs on the best-effort path. | At 10 s, with the margin, Chromium's walk pause through the broker fits under runc, on a Kata block volume, and on both gVisor mounts, and its 9.44 s walk on Kata's shared file system takes the best-effort path when the tracker is off there. Every package-install pause with the tracker fits except Chromium's 12.65 s on Kata's shared file system. A flat directory of 1,000,000 entries does not fit on gVisor's default mount or on Kata's shared file system. A 4 GiB file under constant writes went to the best-effort path with no pause on gVisor and Kata. | A lower budget produces more best-effort snapshots. A higher one allows longer pauses, and an interrupt waits until the pause ends. |
| `checkpointChangeTracker` | Operator on the pool | Removes the stat walk from the pause. | Through the broker the steady pause was 0.013 to 0.094 s for Chromium and LLVM on every configuration where `auto` runs it. | The adapter's CPU for events, at 0.4 to 1.4 µs each, and 10 to 104% longer file creation and deletion in the workload (see "Re-validation through the broker"). |

## Node-level snapshots

Node-level copy-on-write snapshots give an atomic capture whose cost does not depend on file count. They need a per-pod volume on a snapshot-capable filesystem. One possible route is generic ephemeral volumes from a local CSI (Container Storage Interface) driver that offers `VolumeSnapshot` on ZFS or Btrfs, in place of `emptyDir`. The capture pass would become a snapshot request, and an uploader with access to a clone of the snapshot would perform the upload pass.

This design defers that route for these reasons:

- It requires node pools on a particular filesystem and a privileged CSI driver.
- Warm pools would move from `emptyDir` to per-pod volumes, which adds provisioning time to every warm pod.
- Each slot has its own workspace volume, so a pod would need one snapshot-capable volume per slot, which multiplies the provisioning by the slot count.
- The uploader needs a mount of the snapshot that the agent pod does not have today.

The adapter's capture step is an internal interface with operations to fix the changed set at one instant and to read that fixed data, so a node-level provider can replace the `FICLONE` and read paths later. Such a provider would read a snapshot outside the slot's view, and it would need its own answer to the file ownership that the broker answers.

## Relationship to other designs

- **[`0145-session-isolation`](../0145-session-isolation/change.md)** gives every slot its own slot container and UID, whole-volume slot mounts, and an adapter-only spool volume (SI decision D8), and gives the adapter the file-descriptor broker through which every pass of this design reads, stats, clones from, watches, and restores a slot's files (SI decision D9). It owns the broker's operations, `statat` included, its roots, its checks, the supervisor's asynchronous worker, the Sweep, and the slot release. This design owns the walk, the index, the racy rule, the tracker's rules, the spool accounting, the reflink probe, the stored format, the restore order, and the checkpoint stagger. Its O2 rule reports this design's metadata-only entries, its O8 requires user namespaces on runc pools, and its O11 places the slot repository's git steps.
- **[`0118-agent-pod-layout`](../0118-agent-pod-layout/change.md)** owns the runtime-visible layout: `/workspace` and the disk-backed `/session`, the two trees this design checkpoints, the adapter's paths under `/adapter`, the volumes with their size limits, the pod-share annotation's volume list, and the removal of the embedded deployment model, which takes IC-9's in-process broker with it.
- **[`0153-workspace-materialization`](../0153-workspace-materialization/change.md)** owns `lenny-materialize`, whose `capture-copy` runs before every checkpoint's pre-copy and whose `restore-complete` runs after every restore of a workspace with plan repositories, the reserved names this design's walk skips, `spoolSizeLimitBytes` with the staging space the slots share on the spool volume, and the `lenny-store-fill` container that fills the warm stores and their pristine trees.
- **[`0139-runtime-protocol`](../0139-runtime-protocol/change.md)** replaces the integration levels with capabilities, so a quiescing runtime is one that declares `checkpoint`, and every other runtime gets the best-effort snapshot. Its O7 (a), settled on 2026-11-12, moves the quiesce frames from `CH-RUNTIMEOPS` to `CH-MSGSOCK`.
- **[`0136-placement`](../0136-placement/change.md)** owns node classes, the separation of runc nodes, user namespaces on the standard class, and node tenancy, on which the runc lever under "Levers" rests.
- **[`0127-egress-and-git`](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md)** moves git into the pod, under its proposal [EG-3](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#steps) (the pod git path), and injects git configuration ([`0127-egress-and-git`, "Git configuration"](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#git-configuration)), which carries the maintenance settings of [decision D6](#decisions) under that design's decision 59. Its decision 61 places the warm store fill in the `lenny-store-fill` container. Its [EG-2](../0127-egress-and-git/change.md#steps) renders the workspace volume's `sizeLimit`, whose value IC-7 changes.
- **[`0147-tenant-derived-runtimes`](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md)** keeps warm repository stores (["Warm stores"](../0147-tenant-derived-runtimes/change.md#warm-stores)) outside the slot, mounted read-only at `/lenny/git-stores` in every slot container, and the helper reconnects them at restore. This design does not capture those stores, and it reduces what a session's own clone costs after the first checkpoint. Its "Fields" table states what a tenant may set on a derived runtime, and it does not list this design's Runtime fields yet.
- **[`0144-session-derive`](../0144-session-derive/change.md)** keeps its routes and modes. This design changes how a derive with `from: latest` stores its starting point, which changes what that design's storage reservation covers.
- **[`0140-runtime-revisions`](../0140-runtime-revisions/change.md)** classifies this design's Runtime fields, as IC-8, "Runtime fields and revisions" states.
- **[`0146-session-lifecycle`](../0146-session-lifecycle/change.md)** owns the session states. Its `dormant` state checkpoints the workspace and releases the pod, which a session under `checkpointPolicy: sealOnly` or `none` never enters, and its suspension releases a pod after a checkpoint, which those policies do not take (IC-8, "Policy").
- **[`0135-message-delivery`](../0135-message-delivery/change.md)** copies a file the agent produces for the user with this design's chunks, packs, grants, catalog, and reclamation ([MD-18](../0135-message-delivery/change.md#md-18-files)), under the owner's decisions A12 to A21 of that design. IC-2 gives every pack a nonce, IC-3 adds its `ExportOutputFile` stream with `ExportAbort` and freezes a part by clone or by copy within the export share, IC-4 its output-file tables and export records, allowed set, and export quota, IC-5 the seal window that the seal checkpoint and the session's exports share and the rendering of the exports' downloads, IC-6 its retention and holds, and IC-7 the export share of the spool. The checkpoint also records that design's consumed message-id set, which IC-4 makes readable on resume.
- **[`0128-forensics-evidence-preservation`](../0128-forensics-evidence-preservation/change.md)** treats a compromised pod's checkpoints as suspect (["Sessions on the pod"](../0128-forensics-evidence-preservation/change.md#sessions-on-the-pod)). S2 and S4 limit what such a checkpoint can reference or substitute. They do not make its content trustworthy. Its security holds copy held snapshots to escrow and require `EnsureCopied` on every deletion path, which IC-6, "Security holds" states for packs, snapshots, and output files. Its quarantine relies on IC-9, which removes the only specification rule that asks for a liveness probe on an agent pod.

The BarrierAck floor and the gateway's preStop wait scale with the slot count (IC-7, "Drain caps"). | IC-1, IC-7. |

## Costs

- **Postgres.** Each attempt writes one row per new pack and one row per referenced pack. A 5 GiB workspace in 16 MiB packs references about 320 packs per snapshot.
- **Adapter CPU.** SHA-256 ran at 1.1 to 1.7 GiB/s on one core of the measured x86 nodes and at 2.6 GiB/s on the arm64 laptop. zstd at its fastest level compressed at 0.85 to 2.0 GiB/s on one core. The tracker's event reader cost 0.4 to 1.4 µs of CPU per file system event through the broker, against 20 to 60 µs for the adapter-held tracker of "Benchmarks". The walk's `statx` calls run on the adapter's CPU. The file-descriptor broker adds a request for every 253 entries the adapter opens, creates, or watches, and for every 253 entries the walk's fallback stats (IC-1, "The walk"). The supervisor serves those requests on the slot's CPU share.
- **Adapter memory and disk.** The file index holds about 128 bytes per file, so 500,000 files take about 64 MiB. The adapter stores the index as a file under `/adapter/spool/<n>/`, where it counts against the session's spool cap. The re-validation harness held its index in memory less compactly: its adapter's resident memory was 1.0 to 1.3 GiB with Chromium's index and reached 2.0 GiB after a package install, so the 128 bytes per file are a target the implementation has to meet, and the adapter's memory limit has to leave room for one index per slot. The spool volume reserves `spoolSizeLimitBytes` of disk per pod, 16 GiB by default whatever the slot count, of which the adapter uses half (IC-7). Decision D3's 10 GiB workspace default raises each slot's reserved disk, and the namespace ResourceQuota grows with it (IC-7, "Default and rendering"). The adapter-held tracker of "Benchmarks" used up to 480 MiB during setup and 35 to 130 MiB afterwards for 17,000 to 47,000 watched directories.
- **Complexity.** The design adds a storage format, a catalog with reference-based garbage collection, a format conversion for the download route, a broker client with its reply checks and raw-byte path encoder, and the parallel stat walk, about 330 lines of Go in the adapter. The broker itself, `statat` included, adds to the C supervisor, which `0145-session-isolation` counts. It removes the partial-manifest reassembly path and the tiered drain table.
- **Storage.** Retained bytes fall to the unique content of the latest 2 snapshots and of the session's output files, plus sparse-pack overhead bounded by `packRewriteThreshold`. An output file keeps every pack it references alive for the session's retention, so a sparse pack that a rotated snapshot shared with an output file is not compacted away until the output file goes, and its unused chunks are stored for that time.
